New wave of bomb threats hit European airlines

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

European airlines have again been hit with a new wave of bomb threats - in the third such incident this week.

On Friday multiple flights to and from Lisbon, Rome, Prague and Sofia were targeted and the threats saw three planes escorted by Hungarian fighter jets, authorities said.

It also saw a number of high-profile media organisations, including Ireland's public broadcaster RTÃ, evacuated after threats were made against them - although there has been no concrete links between the messages against airlines and the broadcasters.

The disruption comes after nearly 30 aircraft and several airports across Europe were impacted by threats earlier this week, in what the UK called a "malicious hoax".

Russian extremist group denies sending hoax bomb threats

A Ryanair flight scheduled to leave Lisbon for Rome on Friday was targeted with a threat, the airline said, while at least 11 flights were targeted in the Czech Republic and Bulgaria, according to AFP.

France also reported at least four of its flights were targeted by threats, however they all landed safely.

No explosives have been found on any planes as of Friday.

As airlines were dealing with this latest wave of threats, several media outlets, including RTÃ, were evacuated after similar warnings were made against them.

Staff at the Irish Independent newspaper and the Dublin offices of radio stations NewstalkFM and TodayFM, were also advised to leave their buildings and UK broadcaster Sky said it also received a security threat.

In a statement, gardaÃ­ (Irish police) said they were "aware of a threat made against media organisations here and in Europe".

Armed gardaÃ­ were deployed to RTÃ'S Dublin headquarter after a bomb threat

While no link has been established between the threats against airlines and media outlets, the Irish government has warned it is a "likely coordinated campaign of disruption".

In a statement, it said: "Hoax threats such as these cause fear, distress and disruption to people going about their daily lives and activities; they achieve no positive purpose whatsoever. It is not known yet who is responsible for these threats, whether it is individuals or a centralised or co-ordinated effort.

Irish Prime Minister MicheÃ¡l Martin described the situation as "worrying" and added his government's first priority was the safety of the Irish public.

"Yesterday, there appeared to be coordinated efforts to disrupt air travel," he said.

Martin told RTÃ it was not yet known if the threats had come from individuals or if it was a "coordinated attack" but said they were being taken seriously.

Ireland is scheduled to host two high-profile European Union meetings in Dublin next week.

Broadcaster Sky confirmed it had received a security threat, hours after RTÃ had evacuated its building.

The broadcaster, which is owned by Comcast, said in a statement read by a presenter on its news channel that the broadcaster had "carried out a security assessment and reported the matter to the relevant authorities".

"The safety and security of our colleagues is our primary concern, and we are working closely with authorities to ensure our people and operations remain secure."

Friday's disruption comes after the Russian extremist group "Male State", named in this week's hoax bomb threats to Italian airports, denied any link to the false warnings.

Replying to the BBC Russian Service, an account on Telegram - one of several linked to the so-called "Male State" and its founder Vladislav Pozdnyakov - mocked any suggestion the group was involved in emailing the threats.

"What Male State? Where have you seen it? It hasn't existed for many years," the message on the channel reads. "If tomorrow they sign a [threat] letter, say, from Putin, will you then try to reach our president to ask your stupid question?"

Four midterms takeaways for 2028 White House race

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Democratic Senator Jon Ossoff and Republican Vice-President JD Vance

Ballots are still being counted in the US midterm elections but the political landscape is already pivoting to the 2028 White House race.

Buoyed by major gains in Congress, Democrats will have momentum as they prepare for the contest to succeed President Donald Trump.

But a good showing in the midterms does not guarantee a White House win two years later. Barack Obama was re-elected in 2012 despite huge Republican gains in 2010.

No one has yet entered the presidential race but potential candidates will be preparing behind the scenes.

Vice-President's JD Vance's reflection on where the midterms went wrong for Republicans has been interpreted as him paving the way for a 2028 run.

The results of Tuesday's election and the historic size of Democratic gains could shape the White House race in several ways. Here are four takeaways.

Several prominent potential presidential candidates were running in the 2026 midterms - and some emerged looking strong.

Georgia Democratic Senator Jon Ossoff won by a sizeable margin in the politically competitive state that Trump won in 2024.

While he has previously said he has no plans to run for president, his successful campaign, with several viral moments, helped boost his national profile.

Another potential Democratic nominee, Pennsylvania Governor Josh Shapiro, swept aside his Republican challenger.

Shapiro has not committed to a 2028 run but analysts point to his broad appeal in swing-state Pennsylvania as a sign that he could be competitive on the national stage.

Illinois Governor JB Pritzker also won after a campaign pundits in his state described as a "tune-up" for a presidential run.

California Governor Gavin Newsom could not run for re-election in the midterms but he is invested in the outcome of a ballot initiative to tax the state's wealthy residents which he campaigned against.

On the Republican side, there were two potential 2028 names running in Arkansas.

Republican Senator Tom Cotton and Governor Sarah Huckabee Sanders - a one-time Trump administration press secretary - were able to stay in office by a narrower margin than before, both earning just above half of the votes in the conservative state.

Trump names Stephen Miller as new chief of staff

What Democrats can and can't do with their new power after taking Congress

Exit polls suggest the swing against Republicans was partly due to anger felt by voters at the actions of the Trump administration.

That could present a challenge to his potential successors who come from the ranks of the current leadership, including Vice-President JD Vance and Secretary of State Marco Rubio.

"The one person who has been hurt badly by this election campaign is Vance," said Karen Beckwith, a professor of political science at Case Western Reserve University in Ohio.

Vance has already sought to distance himself from defeat - and from a foreign policy adventure in Iran that was politically disastrous for his party.

"The reason we lost is the war," he wrote on X.

Rubio has denied reports he is considering leaving his post ahead of a 2028 presidential run.

Other possible Republican candidates may benefit from not being part of the administration. Some have been staking out a position on what went wrong and what next.

In a lengthy post on X, Senator Ted Cruz, who was not running this year, said that too many Republican politicians seem like "they didn't care" about the economic issues faced by voters.

"It is dark right now, but it is always darkest before the dawn, and morning is coming," he wrote. "That is my fervent prayer, and that is my commitment to you."

Florida Governor Ron DeSantis, who abandoned his 2024 presidential run after being trounced by Trump early on, was similarly critical and pointed to Republican victories in Florida as a sign of what can be accomplished.

White House denies Hegseth and Rubio to leave jobs after Republican election losses

Vance walks Trump tightrope with scathing election critique blaming Iran war

Both the centrist and left wings of the Democratic Party have claimed victory from the results as each side sifts through possible 2028 contenders.

Dozens are already making moves, Obama's former adviser Jim Messina has told the BBC.

El-Sayed's win in Michigan was a breakthrough moment for left-wing Democrats because he triumphed in a swing state.

His supporters now claim his win could supercharge left-wing candidates in the 2028 election.

"The old notions of electability are out the door, and bold economist populist outsiders proved they can win in battleground states," said Adam Green, the co-founder of the Progressive Change Campaign Committee.

Green added that the "water is now warm" for candidates such as New York Democratic representative Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez to run in presidential primaries and "know that they can win."

Moderate Democrats, however, claimed victories across the country, with the "Blue Dog Caucus" of moderate and conservative Democrats gaining at least 14 seats in Congress, nearly doubling its current size.

This has led many within that wing of the party to conclude that centrist candidates that bill themselves as pragmatic and willing to work with conservatives could woo Republican voters.

Given the success of Democratic candidates who focused on cost of living issues, an economy-first approach will likely be a focus again in two years.

Amirah Siquierah, the director of federal affairs at the Working Families Party, said successful candidates in 2028 will combine economic populism with newer concerns such as data centres.

Dozens of Democrats eyeing 2028 presidential run, says former Obama aide

Both parties must contend with a changing political map and a shifting coalition of support for 2028.

Once "safe" Republican parts of the country can no longer be considered conservative strongholds.

Texas is left with only one Republican at a statewide level, which means its 40 electoral college votes could be up for grabs in 2028.

Christina Pascucci, a political commentator and one-time Senate candidate in California, said the midterms "challenge long-held political assumptions" and show that "neither party can afford to take its traditional strongholds for granted".

That is true of both Democrats and Republicans who will need to win over swing voters, added Pascucci, now the CEO of Atlas Global, a global communications and advisory firm.

"Neither party owns these voters," she said.

Texas has a new Democratic governor, Gina Hinojosa

The Trump coalition of working class and Latino voters faltered, reversing many of the gains he had made with those electorates in his successful 2024 campaign.

Driena Sixto, a former spokesperson for Turning Point USA and a conservative political commentator, told the BBC that she believed that Republicans will have to change their messaging to Latino voters ahead of 2028 for any future candidate to be able to reverse the trend.

"Last time, the campaign message was one of safe communities and fighting back against trafficking," she said.

"After the president was sworn in, I think a lot of messaging from the White House did too much to highlight deportations. That was the divide."

AI stocks climb after trillions wiped from market values

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Shares in artificial intelligence and technology firms have recovered slightly from an enormous sell-off that wiped trillions of dollars off stock values.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq stock index closed in positive territory following a market rout that began in the US on Thursday before spilling over to Asian and European markets into Friday.

Investors had been selling off tech shares in part due to concerns the Democrat victory in the midterm elections would mean slower artificial intelligence development.

However the latest US employment figures added to a mixed picture for American markets on Friday morning, with an unexpectedly strong 165,000 jobs added to the economy in September.

On Thursday, businesses closely linked to artificial intelligence development took a hammering on stock markets, particularly chip and semiconductor makers.

But the Nasdaq Composite bounced back slightly on Friday, ending the day up 1.1%, partly reversing some of its 5.5% fall.

The S&P 500, which tracks the share prices of the largest publicly-traded US companies, closed up 0.3%, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average was flat through the day before ending slightly down 0.4%.

Companies that lost hundreds of billions in market value recovered some ground on Friday, including Nvidia, SpaceX and Tesla.

However, stock markets in Asia and Europe fared worse.

The Kospi in South Korea closed down 6% on Friday, with shares in chipmaker SK Hynix plunging more than 10%. European markets also fell, with the main indexes in Germany, France and Spain ending the day down.

In the UK, the FTSE 100, which tracks the largest companies on the London Stock Exchange, declined 1.01%.

In addition to the recent market volatility, UK traders were also digesting the latest Bank of England decision to raise interest rates and the signal that another rate rise could be on the cards to curb rising prices.

The market fluctuations also come after the sweeping Democrat victory in the midterm elections.

A number of Democrats campaigned for new rules and regulations for artificial intelligence (AI) and data centres, and the party has since won control of both houses of congress as well as key contests in states that have been part of the AI data centre race.

US President Donald Trump on Friday reiterated his supported for what he calls "super intelligence", saying only "foolish leadership" would "say no to the riches they bring".

However, there have also been concerns over whether the enormous amounts of money being spent on AI will pay off.

Russ Shaw, founder of global tech advocacy firm Tech London Advocates, said the election result was sending a message that a rethink was needed on AI.

"The incoming Congress is going to really focus on AI in a very dramatic way," he told the BBC's Business Today.

"I'm a big fan of AI, but I'm watching what these companies are doing, and they're not communicating effectively at all, particularly in the US, about what the benefits of AI are all about."

Pierre BaquÃ©, chief executive of AI platform Neural Concept, said the share movements were a "necessary market correction" that reflected a legitimate concern about the amount of money being poured into AI infrastructure.

"The industry must move beyond unchecked infrastructure investment and demonstrate greater financial discipline," he said.

"We are entering a critical transition phase, a market purgatory where raw tech must prove its widespread economic value."

Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at AJ Bell, said it had been a brutal week for markets, and the key question was whether the jump in the S&P 500 and Nasdaq would be sustained.

"Once investors have had the weekend to catch their breath, next week could give a clearer indication of whether the tech trade is going down the pan or fighting back."

Could the US midterms put a pin in the AI bubble?

Elon Musk appears to signal the end of the road for Cybertruck

As well as the fallout of the midterms and the plunge in artificial intelligence stocks, US traders will also be digesting the latest jobs figures.

And those numbers were better than expected: US employers added 165,000 new jobs in October, well above the average monthly increase and more than double what analysts had expected.

Overall the unemployment rate, which counts the number of people who are out of work but actively looking for a job, eased slightly to 4.1% from 4.2% in September.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) said the unemployment rate has remained in the narrow range of 4.1% to 4.3% since March.

Neil Birrell, chief investment officer at investment firm Premier Miton, said while the jobs figures may feel like second billing to the market movements they were still important.

"The US economy is running hard which may mean the risks the markets are worrying about at the moment are not such a risk to the real economy as is being feared," he said.

Axon Plunged To a 2-Year Low. Here's Why It Could Be a Golden Buying Opportunity

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Axon stock tumbled on its Q3 report as it badly missed revenue estimates.

Axon Enterprise (AXON -18.18%), the law enforcement technology company known for Taser electrical weapons and body cameras, tumbled 18% on Friday after it reported third-quarter earnings.

At issue was a significant revenue miss and downbeat guidance for the fourth quarter. In the third quarter, revenue rose 28% to $908.6 million, well below the consensus at $934.3 million. For the fourth quarter, it called for revenue of $990 million-$1.01 billion, up 25%, which would be its slowest revenue growth since the end of 2021, and was below the consensus at $1.07 billion.

However, the revenue miss was due to a timing issue rather than weak demand or something more structural. The company said that the timing of a $30 million Federal Dedrone deployment was expected to be recognized in the third quarter, but was delayed.

As you can see from the numbers above, if it had booked that revenue, the company would have matched revenue estimates.

In addition to the Dedrone revenue being pushed out, there were some other items that left some doubts among investors.

Piper Sandler noted that gross margin in its products division, which includes Taser, was below 50%, and said that the quarter may have been Axon's worst after Q3 2025, which led to a sharp sell-off in the stock. Craig-Hallum downgraded the stock to neutral, citing weak performance at Taser and quarter-to-quarter volatility associated with federal contracts being pushed out.

In addition to the weak revenue result, Axon cut its free cash flow guidance for the year from $450 million to $350 million-$400 million, due to accelerated inventory growth to improve product availability and supply chain resilience.

The company noted lower legacy Taser volume as revenue in connected devices rose 16.3% and adjusted gross margin fell from 52.1% to 44.6% due to increased mix of counter-drone equipment and higher memory costs. Taser sales were down 2% from the quarter a year ago.

Axon just introduced Taser Ion, its most flexible Taser device yet, which should help boost sales of the device.

The company's fourth-quarter guidance was below expectations because it no longer includes two Federal Dedrone deployments worth $100 million.

Management was careful to explain on the earnings call that the dynamic it's dealing with regarding lumpy one-time revenue payments with Federal Dedrone contracts is new. It said it would take a more conservative approach with guidance and better communicate the impact of timing-sensitive hardware.

However, the company defended its demand as CFO Joshua Isner said, "We're calling this out very specifically because I don't want it to obscure what we're seeing in the rest of the business because the demand picture is still very, very strong."

With the 18% drop on Friday, Axon is trading at its lowest point in more than two years, and the stock is now down 64% from its all-time high.

The sell-off makes sense if this business is facing a new set of challenges or its growth prospects are now diminished. However, that doesn't seem to be the case. Aside from the Dedrone delay, revenue is where the company expected it to be, and Axon continues to invest in the future, including a $1.2 billion investment in Skydio, a leading drone technology company, adding to its existing investment and bringing its stake in the company to 37%.

Axon's strategy historically has paid off, and there aren't any red flags here. The stock now trades at a price-to-sales ratio of 7, which is a good value for a company with a track record of growing at 30% annually and is set to do it again this year.

Investors who take advantage of the sell-off are likely to be rewarded in time.

Jeremy Bowman has positions in Axon Enterprise. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Axon Enterprise. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

$2,000 for black bars: The worst thing about the iPhone Duo isn’t made by Apple

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Some very high profile apps look downright embarrassing

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I like to think I’m a patient person, but now that we’re two weeks after the iPhone Duo release date, I’m starting to wonder what the heck is going on with the app situation.

It’s not like the iPhone Duo doesn’t have other issues, whether it’s “Crackgate,” the weighty design, or the lack of a telephoto zoom. But my biggest issue right now has nothing to do with Apple or the hardware.

Don’t get me wrong; there are plenty of apps that have already been updated and optimized for Apple’s first foldable phone. Some of my favorite iPhone Duo apps include YouTube TV, which allows you to watch four shows at once in a grid view, the PianoFold Rhythm game, and AirMail with its handy 2-column layout.

But overall I’m pretty shocked by how many high-profile apps still look awful on the $2,000 iPhone Duo 2. Here are some of the worst offenders, and I’m warning you now that several are from Google.

I use Google Drive all of the time for uploading photos and reviewing documents, but I simply don’t want to on the iPhone Duo because it’s so visually jarring. There are huge black bars on the left and right on the 7.6-inch display, and the buttons to rotate and enlarge the view don’t really help.

You would think that spreadsheets would look way better on the iPhone Duo’s main screen. And they would if the app was actually optimized. Instead, you get the same black bars here as in the Google Drive app, and you have to scroll a lot to see content. It looks totally unoptimized.

Here’s another app that fills up 1/3 of the screen. Granted, I mostly use Uber on the cover display for booking rides, but Lyft has already updated and optimized its app — so it fills the big screen.

Sign up to the Tom's iGuide weekly newsletter with coverage on everything from product launches to software updates, this is your go-to source for the latest updates on all the best Apple content.

Look, I’m not expecting an immersive LinkedIn experience when I use my iPhone Duo, but I’m not expecting a layout that looks outdated and broken. Pressing the rotate button fills up more of your screen, but it’s still not great. Maybe LinkedIn could post an app developer job ad…

I don’t have a major problem using the 5.4-inch cover display for ordering dinner. But I would at least like to have the option to use the bigger canvas for browsing menus, especially Cheesecake Factory.

There are some pretty great calendar apps for the iPhone Duo. Google Calendar is not one of them. For now I would download Fantastical. It offers a wide format on the big screen and lets you easily check the weather along with your schedule and keep track of your to-dos.

This isn’t the only banking app that hasn’t been updated for the iPhone Duo yet, but it’s annoying for me because it’s the one I use. Wells Fargo is fine on the front screen for checking your balance or doing a quick transfer, but not so much for reviewing purchases where it helps to have more real estate.

The Gmail app looks better than Drive and Sheets in that it actually fills the screen (yay!), but it doesn’t feature the 2-column layout that I would expect. So you can’t see your inbox on the left and your messages on the right as you can on Android foldables. It’s a massive waste of a 7.6-inch canvas.

Did you hear that Doordash is buying Instacart for $17 billion? Well, it appears that neither company can afford to optimize their apps for the iPhone Duo right now. You basically get the phone app slapped on a larger screen, which makes shopping and browsing more tedious than it needs to be.

While there are plenty of apps that look great on the iPhone Duo, too many apps are just not optimized yet. And while it’s not Apple’s responsibility to fix the problem, the longer this drags on the more excuses people will have not to splurge. I would be way more aggressive in cajoling developers to update their wares for this form factor.

Maybe app makers are waiting for a critical mass of iPhone Duo users, which is a chicken-and-egg scenario that may be hard to overcome — at least until Apple is able to keep up with demand for this device.

Archaeologists unearthed a Pompeii cupboard containing 38 coins and a bag of childhood charms that were buried in the 79 AD eruption

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

A Pompeii cupboard buried in 79 AD held 38 bronze coins, 36 from Nero's reign, 24 childhood charms in a cloth bag and, nearby, two young wood mice.

In a back room of House IX 11, 7 on Via dell’Abbondanza, archaeologists found the imprint of a wooden Pompeii cupboard in the volcanic ash.

The Pompeii Archaeological Park says it was never reopened after Mount Vesuvius erupted in 79 AD. Inside were 38 bronze coins in a basket, 24 small charms in a cloth bag, tweezers, a lamp and pots.

The park put out the news on October 29, 2026, with a 37-page report by director Gabriel Zuchtriegel and six colleagues. They do not know who lived there or what happened to them.

The front of the house was dug in 1912. Inscriptions around the door gave the name Cornelius Maximus. They can no longer be seen, and the report calls the name very doubtful.

The back rooms were reached only now, in the last phase of a project on the neighboring Insula dei Casti Amanti, the city block named for the House of the Chaste Lovers.

The goal was to make the slope safe along the alley between blocks 11 and 12, so old metal scaffolding could come down.

The top layer was modern fill with plastic and bits of metal. Under it the team found a toilet built into a wall niche in room D. Room C held a satyr-face roof tile and a bronze pot.

Room B, about 14 by 12.6 feet (4.3 by 3.85 meters), was probably a dining room. Its yellow walls show birds, panthers, a peacock and dolphins. Domenico Esposito, of the Freie Universität Berlin, put them down to the painters of Via di Castricio, a known workshop.

Building or repair work was going on in the house when the volcano erupted, the report says. Upper floors rarely survive at Pompeii, and researchers have used staircases that lead nowhere in the House of Thiasus to work out what stood above.

Two tunnels had been cut into these rooms after the eruption, at a date the report cannot fix. The second, about 3.6 feet (1.1 meters) wide, came from the west and ended at the east wall of room B.

It wrecked the fill in the middle of the room but left the ash along the sides. In that ash, the team found the shape of a big cupboard in the southeast corner.

It had two parts, each about 6.6 feet (2 meters) long. One was about 3 feet (0.9 meters) deep and the other 2.3 feet (0.7 meters). Two iron pieces fixed it to the floor.

The roof came down and broke the top of the cupboard. Then a pyroclastic flow, a fast current of hot ash and gas, came in from the northwest and filled it. More than half of its height survived.

The cupboard held five jugs, flasks, cooking pots, a glass plate, a bronze lamp and an incense burner. A thin cup had a pine-cone-scale pattern. There was also a glass ointment bottle, bronze tweezers and a slate palette for grinding makeup.

In the south part was a small wooden box with a bronze catch. It held eight lead weights, six with iron handles, and the authors say these may have been used for trade.

About 8 inches (0.2 meters) from the box lay a lump of 38 bronze coins. They had been kept in a little basket of woven plant fiber. Only part of its bottom, about 4.7 inches (0.12 meters), was left.

Two coins were old and worn, one from Ebusus, now Ibiza, and one a sestertius of Claudius struck in the name of Germanicus.

The other 36 were struck under Nero between 64 and 67 AD: 26 asses, seven dupondii and three sestertii, all Roman coin denominations. Twenty-one are from 65 AD.

The report calls it a small reserve, maybe for day-to-day spending. The team did not harden the basket with consolidant, so other tests could still be done. Archaeobotanist Chiara Comegna looked at it under a stereo microscope in the park’s lab.

About 2 feet (0.6 meters) from the east edge, along the inner south wall, the team found 24 tiny objects in faience, bone, glass paste and rock crystal. Each had a hole for a string.

There are the gods Bes and Mercury, hands, feet, pine cones or grapes, beads, a knucklebone and a winged shoe.

Romans called these crepundia, from a Latin word for rattling. Children wore them as protection, and adults could hear them moving about. Some earlier finds like these were taken for magic kit.

The bag sat on a middle shelf. A post about 6 inches (0.15 meters) thick fell on that shelf, the shelf tilted, and the bag slid to the back and tore. Under the microscope the cloth shows a regular weave at about 0.04 inches (1 millimeter).

Adults sometimes kept these necklaces all their lives, and at the Porta Nocera cemetery they were put in graves. The park notes that many Romans lived far from their parents and childhood homes.

Zuchtriegel said the find lets the team read “the different traces of a life” together: “personal habits, work, memories.”

In small bedroom B1 next door, seven post holes about 6.6 feet (2 meters) up the walls showed where a wooden loft had been. On it sat a North African olive oil amphora, six lids, three flasks, two pots and a big storage jar.

The bottom of the broken amphora was sieved and gave 117 tiny bones. Zooarchaeologist Younes Naime put them in the genus Apodemus, most likely the wood mouse. Naime counted two animals at the very least. Neither had finished growing.

Wood mice normally live in fields and gardens, the park says, but they slip into attics and food stores whenever they smell a meal. The house mouse is a different species, and it has followed people around since before farming began.

Back in 2023, the park described two mice and a young black rat found in the slave quarters of the Civita Giuliana villa. Rodent bones have also come out of the old sewers of Herculaneum.

Did the people of the house die in the eruption? Or did they run, and never make it back for their things? The report admits the evidence cannot settle it, and that is fair.

The press release mentioned Tacitus, who wrote that property left without heirs was put to use after the disaster. The report itself plays that down.

That link needs more study before anyone ties it to this house, and wall paintings alone say little about how rich a family was.

DNA tests on Pompeii’s victims have shown how wrong old guesses about sex and family can be. Other finds have pointed to the public role of women in the city.

“We cannot reconstruct with certainty the identity of whoever lived in the house,” Zuchtriegel said. Still to come: a textile specialist for the cloth samples, and more digging along the north and south walls of room B, which are only partly cleared.

The coins and the bag of charms were still inside the cupboard when the ash filled it in 79 AD.

The full study was published in the journal E-Journal degli Scavi di Pompei.

Forget 'Se7en,' Jake Gyllenhaal's Apple TV Thriller 'Prisoners' Is Even Better

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

A 150-minute-plus, dark-as-night kidnapping thriller isn't an instant recipe for box office success, yet Denis Villeneuve's 2013 film Prisoners handily made almost three times its budget back, while also landing on numerous year-end Top 10 lists. And the sprawling, dour crime film is still proving to have legs, as it's currently rocketing up the charts on Apple TV.

While Prisoners doesn't have any actual supernatural elements, Villeneuve and master cinematographer Roger Deakins (Blade Runner 2049, The Assassination of Jesse James by the Coward Robert Ford) conjure such an enveloping atmosphere of menace that when villainous characters who may actually be related to a Satanic cult are introduced, it doesn't come as much of a shock at all. The film is long, unpleasantly themed, and generally hopeless. It's also a modern masterpiece, and currently receiving its due ahead of Villeneuve's eagerly anticipated Dune Part Three.

The story of Prisoners actually dates back to 2009, when Aaron Guzikowski penned a script based on his own short story, making that year's Black List. It was shopped around to stars and directors, reportedly including Leonardo DiCaprio and Antoine Fuqua, before landing in the capable hands of Denis Villeneuve. Fresh off the critically acclaimed (and extremely dark) dramatic thrillers Polytechnique and Incendies, Villeneuve proved the perfect match for the story of suburban Connecticut dad Keller Dover (Hugh Jackman), whose young daughter, along with his neighbor's child, vanishes into thin air on a chilly Thanksgiving. While Jake Gyllenhaal's Detective Loki works the case, Dover takes matters into his own hands.

Prisoners takes on an even darker tone once Dover becomes convinced that a young man with an intellectual disability, played masterfully by Paul Dano, is the culprit and takes matters into his own hands. Torture, amateur investigation, false leads, and an apparent occult kidnapping conspiracy dating back decades take over the final half, as the film keeps viewers' hearts pounding for over two and a half hours.

The story of Prisoners is fairly standard kidnapping thriller material (until it takes some dark and, arguably, unnecessary turns toward the occult and conspiratorial), but it's elevated by the sheer mood and craft that Villeneuve and Deakins bring to its world. The opening disappearance sequence is a perfect model of grey, chilly suburban banality that's nevertheless suffused with menace. Deakins lights abandoned buildings, streetlights, and parking lots like they're nestled somewhere within one of Dante's seven layers of hell. Lighting aside, Deakins' still, observational frames at time evoke another all-timer of a thriller featuring Gyllenhaal, Zodiac.

And frankly, despite its rather conventional story beats, Prisoners is rivaled by precious few American thrillers of the past several decades, including Zodiac, The Silence of the Lambs, and Se7en, a film from which it apparently borrows much of its hopeless, Satanic atmosphere. Jackman's role has been turned into a meme by many at this point, but his anguished father is actually astonishingly visceral work. In the film's famous torture sequence, in which Dover tries in vain to get information out of Dano's intellectually disabled suspect, Jackman reportedly improvised a few brutal swings of his hammer — with one landing so close to Dano's head it legitimately terrified the actor and caused him to cower to the floor.

Standing proudly alongside Se7en and The Silence of the Lambs as a potboiler elevated to high art through the magic of cinematic craftsmanship, Prisoners remains a compelling rewatch today. It's no wonder audiences are flocking to it on Apple TV.

The Vikings could be ready to make a trade for a quarterback, if they lose Monday night

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Kyler Murray or Carson Wentz? Carson Wentz or Kyler Murray?

This week’s apparent soft benching of Murray, who has a hamstring injury that possibly wouldn’t have kept him from playing if the Vikings had beaten the Lions on Sunday, gives Wentz a chance to beat the 6-1 Bills on Monday night. If he pulls it off, Wentz could keep the job for the remainder of the four-game gjallarhorn gauntlet that ends with trips to Wisconsin to play the Packers and Mexico to play the 49ers.

If Wentz fails, the Vikings may mobilize. Immediately.

The Vikings will have roughly 16 hours after Monday night’s game ends to close a deal with another team for a quarterback. On Monday, we made the case for a trade for Colts quarterback Daniel Jones. As the week has unfolded, a variey of indications have emerged that it’s a real possibility.

Jones, who surely is dismayed by the Colts’ decision to prematurely throw in the towel by trading away guard Quenton Nelson and defensive tackle DeForest Bucker, would likely welcome the chance to return to a team with which he spent a couple of months in 2024 — and to lead the effort to propel the neo-Purple People Eaters to the postseason.

The other possibility would be to try to bring Kirk Cousins home. That would be more difficult. At 4-4, the Raiders are on the fringes of contention. Win or lose on Sunday at San Francisco, why disrupt something that’s working? Cousins currently ranks third in passing yards with 2,124 and second in touchdown passes with 19. His passer rating of 101.4 is higher than Josh Allen’s.

Even if the Raiders would be inclined to move Cousins and ride with Fernando Mendoza, it would cost far less to get Jones than it would to get Cousins. Especially since the Colts may already be planning to cut Jones before a $4 million roster bonus comes due in March and $6.51 million of his $33.49 million salary becomes fully-guaranteed.

With a new G.M. coming next year, why wouldn’t the Colts want a clean slate? Doing so would wipe out $38 million in non-guaranteed cash for 2027. Trading Jones would result in getting value; cutting Jones would get them nothing.

It may all come down to Monday night. If Minnesota wins, chances are nothing happens. If Minnesota loses, Tuesday could become a very interesting day.

Narnia’s Most Debated Ending Isn’t the Problem Fans Think It Is

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Anyone who has thoroughly read through C.S. Lewis' The Chronicles of Narnia series will probably know the basics of the story and how religion played a big role in shaping it. They will also be aware that the final novel, The Last Battle, is missing one pivotal character: Susan Pevensie.

For years, many have criticized Lewis for his decision to leave the daughter of Eve out of Aslan's Country, a decision that the author must have known would rile folks up in the end. But while this creative choice was certainly intentional, it is by no means the "problem" that authors like Philip Pullman claim it to be. In fact, it proves that they, like Susan herself, entirely missed the point, and her absence is much more fitting when properly contextualized.

Near the end of the novel, the Earth-based "Friends of Narnia" — Peter, Edmund, and Lucy, their cousin Eustace, his friend Jill, their mentor Digory Kirke, and his childhood friend Polly Plummer — arrive to help King Tirian at the end of Narnian history. Peter banishes the demon Tash just before Aslan ushers all who were faithful to him into Aslan's Country. Not only do we discover that Susan is absent from the group, but we later learn that all but she were killed in a train accident in their world, including Mr. and Mrs. Pevensie. This is why Aslan allows them all to stay in his heavenly realm. Of course, this also means that Susan, who has since turned her back on Narnia, is now without her family in the "real world."

At the end of Prince Caspian, Aslan had told Peter and Susan that they would not return to the fantasy world. While Peter still clung to those years they served as Narnian royalty, Susan let them go entirely. In the twelfth chapter of The Last Battle, Eustace reveals that Susan now views Narnia as "funny games [they] used to play" during childhood. Jill further describes her priorities as "nothing nowadays except nylons and lipstick and invitations." Lastly, Polly adds that the former Queen of Narnia had "wasted all her school time wanting to be the age she is now," stating that she ought to truly grow up in a more authentic sense. Whatever your initial feelings on the matter, Lewis uses this revelation as a moment to pause and reflect on something he has actually been trying to say since the first Narnian chronicle.

Lewis famously dedicated The Lion, the Witch and the Wardrobe to his goddaughter, Lucy Barfield. Although he finished the book too late for her to enjoy it in childhood, he hoped that she would one day “be old enough to start reading fairy tales again.” It is with this idea in mind that we must reflect on Susan, who seemingly grew too “adult” for Narnia by maturing into a woman who could not leave the Shadowlands behind. While common critiques suggest that Lewis simply misunderstands Susan's mature desires, her decision is not (contrary to popular belief) about sex, nor is it an inherently misogynistic problem on the author's part. After all, Lucy, Jill, and Polly all remain faithful. Rather, the author juxtaposes the Friends' longing for "another country" (the spiritual) with Susan’s obsession with the tangibility of the present (the material). Instead of reflecting well on the lessons Aslan taught, she let them pass her by.

Tragically, this is largely in step with Susan's priorities throughout the novels. In LWW, for instance, she refuses to entertain Lucy's experience even after Professor Kirke (a "grown-up") suggests she should. Indeed, upon arriving in Narnia, she more than once desires to go back, even wishing to abandon Mr. Tumnus to the White Witch. Susan didn't simply "mature" out of Narnia — it's all rooted in something deeper, and Prince Caspian gives us a clue as to what that is. Here, Susan is famously the last one to see Aslan, refusing again to believe Lucy; even worse, she makes a fuss when the others do. Looking back, it's hard to see all this as anything but foreshadowing for The Last Battle. While Lewis does show us a Susan who embraces her queenly role in A Horse and His Boy, she loses sight of who she was and who crowned her when back on Earth.

Susan always struggled with faith. In Prince Caspian, she confides to Lucy that she "really believed" all along that Aslan had been calling them — or, more accurately, as she admits, "I could have, if I let myself." As Aslan soon tells her, she allowed fear to cloud her faith, keeping her from trusting what she could not see. In the case of The Last Battle, we can't fully know why she chose to reject Narnia. Perhaps it was in fear of seemingly losing Aslan again like before. Of course, if this were the case, one would suspect that Peter, Digory, or Polly (all the eldest of the Friends) would have wrestled with these same fears. Instead, Lewis leans into Susan's pre-established dispositions, perhaps being more true to her character than readers would have preferred.

At the end of The Voyage of the Dawn Treader — in which Susan does not appear — Aslan reveals to Edmund, Lucy, and Eustace the entire point of their fantasy adventures: "...by knowing me here for a little, you may know me better there." This was Lewis' in-text revelation that Aslan was meant to be a "supposal" of how Jesus Christ might appear in another world. In a sense, Narnia was a "spiritual training ground" for the Pevensies, meant to teach them how to fully recognize Christ in their world. As the character who most struggles with the notion of faith (i.e., believing without always seeing), Susan became swept up in unbelief, and thus was unable to grasp the deeper truths of Narnia. For her, "growing up" wasn't really about having more "mature experiences," but a way to escape the pain of losing those years she spent elsewhere.

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

While Peter declares with sorrow that Susan is “no longer a friend of Narnia,” that does not mean she’s been rejected by Aslan as an outcast forever. The book leaves her fate uncertain, but Lewis always believed her story continued. In a 1957 letter collected in C.S. Lewis’ Letters to Children, the author states: “She is left alive in this world at the end, having by then turned into a rather silly, conceited young woman. But there's plenty of time for her to mend, and perhaps she will get to Aslan's Country in the end . . . in her own way.” Lewis would not have cared so little for Susan as to leave her in complete hopelessness. Perhaps, by removing her family from the picture, he was giving her the opportunity to finally reflect on her life and choose faith for herself, finding Aslan through her grief — something Lewis, who would later pen A Grief Observed, knew quite a bit about.

In a 1960 letter recorded in The Collected Letters of C.S. Lewis, the author admits that he couldn't write this story himself. “Not that I have no hope of Susan’s ever getting to Aslan’s Country," he explained, "but because I have a feeling that the story of her journey would be longer and more like a grown-up novel than I wanted to write.” Instead, he encouraged readers to write that story themselves. As Stuart Hunt of Into the Wardrobe points out, Lewis famously struggled with his faith. Though he was raised Anglican, he rejected religion entirely in his younger years before returning to Christianity in his early 30s (with the help of his friend J.R.R. Tolkien). Understanding this, it appears that the author's intent was never to push Susan aside, but to express a part of himself in her that simply could not be explored in these seven books — something many would relate to as they, too, grew up.

Susan Pevensie may be the most tragic character in The Chronicles of Narnia, but she is certainly not without hope. After all, as even Lewis himself once said, "When I became a man I put away childish things, including the fear of childishness and the desire to be very grown up." Of course, we can always fall back on Aslan's own words: "Once a king or queen in Narnia, always a king or queen."

Narnia: The Magician's Nephew hits theaters in 2027.

Sequoia shutting down its Scouts program marks the end of an era

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Sequoia this week announced a broad overhaul in a memo posted to X, a move that includes closing its London office and shutting down its scout program. Observers warn that it would be a mistake to read too much into the London closure, since the British capital has a thriving AI scene. On the other hand, the decision to end scouts says more about where venture investing is headed.

That’s partly because 17 years ago, Sequoia pioneered the scouts model, in which select founders and operators write checks on the firm’s behalf. Amid increased competition for deal flow, many VC firms followed suit. But a consensus now seems to be emerging that the model has reached its limits in its current form.

Not everyone was sold on the model in the first place. “They pioneered the scout program but [to be honest] it never made sense to me!” VC Sheel Mohnot wrote on X this week. “The scouts themselves don’t really care that much about the investments, you can still miss (like they did with Uber), not sure what it gets you. I suspect other firms will shutter theirs too.” he added.

To be clear, Sequoia didn’t make a case against scouts in the memo. Instead, it framed its decision as part of a broader need for more focus. But its goal of “putting more energy behind fewer people and things” is likely to resonate with others who’ve questioned the ‘spray and pray’ approach to scouting.

Rival firm Andreessen Horowitz (a16z), for instance, rolled out a global scout network aimed at spotting opportunities in the U.S. and also in smaller European hubs. But a16z partner Gabriel Vasquez told TechCrunch earlier this year that the firm had revamped its program to double down “on the best people.”

Under this new approach, Vasquez explained, scouts get more resources but are also expected to be more active, such as by co-hosting dinners with the firm. “They get more budget, but we have reduced the number,” he said.

This also seems to indicate that it is VC firms that have been driving an overhaul of the model. For founders, being a scout is attractive: it brings them closer to firms they may want checks from someday, or even a partner role with, and being seen as someone who can write a check can raise their standing among other founders.

Some scouts will undoubtedly mourn the end of Sequoia’s program. Among them is Station F boss Roxanne Varza, who wrote on LinkedIn that she was “sad” to hear that the program was getting cut off, and “hope[d] that other funds [w]on’t follow suit.”

Varza, a former Sequoia scout from 2020 to 2025, told TechCrunch that she had no information beyond what has been shared publicly, but observed that becoming a scout had often been a stepping stone for aspiring investment managers.

“I cannot name all of the incredible scouts that have gone on to join or build funds, but there are quite a few. And the scout programs may have helped them get there — allowed them to build an investment track record, allowed them to build relationships with various funds directly, and more,” she wrote to TechCrunch.

According to Varza, the program’s impact went beyond the individual level, instead helping the entire ecosystem become more mature. “I believe they have helped to foster the investment culture and early-stage investment landscape that we now see in Europe, for example,” Varza said.

While others may agree with Varza about the role these programs have played, they may still have run their course for two reasons. First, seasoned operators may get more financial upside as angels. When it’s their own money, they get to keep the returns, too. Second, leading VC firms are now big and well-known enough that the best founders tend to come to them, so they worry less about missing deals, except at the very earliest stages or in niches and geographies where they have less reach.

Still, the pull of those early and niche deals is why scouts programs are much more likely to get revamped than to disappear entirely. “While Sequoia may be cutting the program, venture partner-style deals are likely to continue,” said Varza in her email to TechCrunch. “Scouts was just a way to scale the format. I don’t imagine Sequoia would end all forms of venture partners, but they are full of surprises.”

'Primetime,' 'Resident Evil,' 'Possible Love,' and more movie night hits just landed on streaming

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

With spooky season in the rearview mirror, we're barreling through November at a steady clip, and another weekend ahead of us means the question of what to watch is fresh on our mind. Luckily, this week came with a wave of premieres on the best streaming services, so you're spoiled for choice.

Over on paid video-on-demand platforms, two of the biggest movies of the year — Robert Pattinson's queasy and mesmerizing turn in "Primetime" and Zach Cregger's surprisingly hilarious "Resident Evil" adaptation — are both available to stream from the comfort of your catch. For fans of "You Can See Everything," Netflix has a new drama called "Possible Love" that scratches the same itch.

So without further ado, let's dive into all the best new movies on streaming this weekend.

Robert Pattinson's been everywhere you look this year, but if you only see one of his blockbusters, I can't recommend "Primetime" enough. Director Lance Oppenheim turns a real-life media scandal into a neo-gothic nightmare that oozes style in every frame. Pattinson is delightfully unnerving as Chris Hansen, the host of the controversial reality series “To Catch a Predator,” which became infamous for its televised sting operations of suspected child predators.

Hansen seems almost giddy about the prospect of publicly shaming his targets, but as he and ambitious producer Andie Bender (Merritt Wever) push for bigger ratings, the public spectacle drowns out any moral standing they might have started with. It all turns into a queasy yet hypnotic critique of our obsession with reality TV, and a chilling reminder that sometimes the people behind the camera are just as terrifying as those in front of it.

Buy or rent "Primetime" on Amazon or Apple now

We're in a golden age of video game movie adaptations, and nowhere was that more on display than in Zach Cregger's new "Resident Evil" movie. Even if you've never played the games, you should still check out this tightly woven and surprisingly hilarious thriller with some of the best monster designs I've seen in years. Austin Abrams had a direct line to my funny bone as Bryan, our bumbling protagonist and audience avatar stuck with taking a package into Raccoon City.

He'll have to dodge armies of the horribly mutated undead to have any hope of getting out of this nightmare alive.

If you were fascinated by "You Can See Everything," then Netflix's new drama from acclaimed Korean filmmaker Lee Chang-dong needs to be on your radar. It's not a documentary, but it is a fictional drama about a documentary that chews over many of the same questions about unreliable narrators and how much we can really trust filmmakers to tell us the truth.

Jeon Do-yeon stars as a a working-class woman named Mi-ok whose husband, Ho-seok (Sul Kyung-gu), was one of several auto plant workers laid off after a union strike where authorities beat and killed workers. Mi-ok befriends a documentary filmmaker Lee Ye-ji (Cho Yeo-jeong) and agrees to be in a documentary about the declining state of labor in South Korea. But as the two get closer, it becomes less and less clear what's real chemistry and what's a show put on for the cameras.

Steam Officially Drops 5 More Free Games You Can Keep Forever (November 7)

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Steam is the best friend to any gamer on a budget. The platform doesn't offer just games that are cheap, it also has a slew of totally free titles for anyone curious to try. Price doesn't always equate to quality when it comes to PC gaming, and there's no harm in trying a free game or two to pass the time. There are always some surprises in store, and the cost couldn't be lower.

Some of the free games on Steam this month are more than worth playing, especially considering their zero-dollar price point. Some horror titles are still slipping through the cracks after October, but there's plenty else available for free on the platform this month. Five of the games made free this week in particular might be more gamers' cup of tea than others, and the only loss in playing them is a little time and hard drive space.

This week, free-to-play gamers can look forward to an mech-based arena shooter, a comedy RPG, an incremental idle game, and more to entertain themselves on a budget.

The first free game on offer is Voltgate, a fast-paced arena shooter with a twist: players can customize their own robots and take to the arena. Voltgate has PvP for two to four players, as well as a four-player co-op wave survival mode called Headliner. This arena shooter should be a quick pick-up for those who want something quick, chaotic, and free to play with their friend group.

Townlet is the next notable free entry this week. A taskbar town idler, Townlet changes and grows based on the types of files you delete and send to your Recycle Bin. This is an interesting gimmick, but not as destructive of one as it seems. Players can simply delete something and watch their villagers grab it and take it away, then restore the file. Townlet is a very low-interaction entry to the idler genre.

Den: A Short Horror Story released in the tail-end of October but deserves a mention. Short and not-so-sweet, Den offers a short camping horror story that one can finish in a single sitting. Those still riding the horror wave will want to try this surprisingly immersive free game.

The fourth free game featured this week is WendyQuest: Legend of the Four Corner Stones, which is tie-in game for the Wendy's fast food restaurants. WendyQuest is a surprisingly long and well-made game, with some players taking up to 12 hours to complete this fast food JRPG packed with personality. It's more than a little goofy, which is just what Wendy would want for her own video game.

Last but certainly not least this week is Massive Army Battle. Akin to Totally Accurate Battle Simulator but with hald-drawn sprites, Massive Army Battle offers 12 levels to play as well as one-on-one PvP. The base game is available for free and has plenty of gameplay on its own, and there is a paid Sandbox DLC to add Sandbox mode, six new heroes, and more. The base game is fun enough on its own, and costs absolutely nothing to play.

These five free Steam games may not be able to hold a candle to this season's big releases, but any one of them might be just the thing for a gamer on a budget to pass the time this month.

6 Airlines Offering Premium Economy On Flights Shorter Than 6 Hours

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

What do passengers expect from a modern airline cabin? Long-haul widebodies typically feature dedicated multi-cabin layouts, while short-haul regional hops stick to strict two-class narrowbodies. However, evolving passenger expectations and fleet-utilization strategies have disrupted that boundary. Across high-density regional and transcontinental corridors, a growing number of major carriers are selling international-grade premium economy seats on sectors lasting under six hours.

The trend provides an intriguing value proposition for travelers heading on three to five-hour flights. Rather than paying a substantial markup for short-haul business class or sacrificing personal space in a high-density economy cabin, flyers can find wider seats, additional legroom, and enhanced dining at a fraction of the cost. Still, as airlines deploy long-haul widebodies and long-range narrowbodies on regional trunk routes, availability often hinges on specific aircraft swaps and seasonal scheduling.

Cathay Pacific has long used its internationally configured widebody fleet to operate high-capacity regional routes across East and Southeast Asia. On major commercial trunks such as the 1,588-mile (2,555 km) corridor between Hong Kong International Airport (HKG) and Singapore Changi Airport (SIN), a flight time averaging roughly four hours, Cathay Pacific routinely schedules Airbus A350-900 aircraft equipped with dedicated 28-seat premium economy cabins. Here, Cathay allows regional business travelers access fixed-shell privacy and upgraded meal services with no long-haul business class ticket prices.

As detailed in a cabin review by Point Hacks, Cathay Pacific’s A350 premium economy layout features a 2-4-2 arrangement providing 40 inches (102 cm) of seat pitch and 19 inches (48 cm) of seat width. The airline also schedules these widebodies on four to five-hour overnight red-eye flights to Japan, Singapore, and Indonesia. Furthermore, as reported by The Alviator, Cathay Pacific has integrated its newly redesigned Boeing 777-300ER premium economy cabins into short-haul regional rotations connecting Hong Kong to Tokyo Haneda Airport (HND), New Chitose Airport (CTS), and Beijing Capital Airport (PEK).

However, when booking regional premium economy on Cathay Pacific, it is important to pay close attention to daily equipment schedules. The carrier regularly rotates regionally configured Airbus A330-300s and regional A350s onto these same intra-Asia routes, which lack a premium economy section entirely.

Air New Zealand frequently deploys its long-haul Boeing 787-9 Dreamliners across the trans-Tasman sea to connect Auckland Airport (AKL) with key Australian hubs like Sydney Kingsford Smith Airport (SYD) and Melbourne Airport (MEL). Ranging from three to four hours, these short-haul international hops feature a dedicated 41 inches (104 cm) seat pitch in premium economy, delivering significantly more legroom than standard domestic offerings.

Analysis published by Simple Flying notes that Air New Zealand’s trans-Tasman premium economy is often the smartest buy for passengers, evaluating price versus comfort. Because the short flight duration makes a full lie-flat bed in Business Premier less essential, the generous recline, dedicated footrests, and premium dining in premium economy deliver most long-haul benefits at a much lower price.

As a result, Air New Zealand can maximize aircraft utilization between major long-haul rotations to North America and Asia. Offering international-grade seating on short daytime hops, the carrier fills mid-cabin capacity with high-yielding business and leisure travelers. What is important to keep in mind is that this regional widebody strategy is not unique to Oceania and is equally prominent across key trunk corridors in Southeast Asia.

More space, better meals, and none of the business class price tag — but only if you know which airline to book.

Singapore Airlines regularly deploys long-haul widebodies across its regional network, connecting its Changi hub with high-density commercial markets across Asia. On the 1,588-mile (2,555 km) trunk route between Singapore and Hong Kong, a flight lasting roughly four hours, the carrier regularly sends its long-haul-configured A350-900 and 777-300ER aircraft. These airframes feature dedicated premium economy cabins offering 38 inches (97 cm) of seat pitch and up to 19.5 inches (50 cm) of seat width, providing a spacious middle-tier option for regional business travelers.

As reported by Simple Flying, Singapore Airlines periodically increases capacity on this four-hour regional corridor by deploying its flagship Airbus A380 during peak travel seasons. Positioned as the shortest A380 route in the carrier's global network, these double-decker superjumbos feature a dedicated main-deck premium economy cabin outfitted in a 2-4-2 layout. Passengers on these short-haul rotations are given full long-haul hardware amenities, including 13.3-inch (34 cm) HD seatback monitors, calf rests, and leather seating finishes.

However, booking a premium economy seat on Singapore Airlines' short-haul routes also comes with frequent aircraft changes. The airline also operates medium-haul A350-900 variants across its regional network, which are configured in a two-class setup that lacks premium economy entirely. Generally, Asian carriers use widebodies to solve regional slot constraints, whereas major US legacy airlines form their fleet on a mix of repositioning widebodies and next-generation narrowbodies to bring premium economy to domestic transcontinental markets.

In North America, Delta Air Lines has introduced its international-grade Delta Premium Select cabin onto select domestic transcontinental corridors. Delta does not limit the product strictly to long-haul transoceanic flights and offers Premium Select on key hub-to-hub transcontinental rotations, such as New York John F. Kennedy International Airport (JFK) to Los Angeles International Airport (LAX) and San Francisco International Airport (SFO). On these 2,475-mile (3,983 km) transcontinental links, flight times vary directionally as eastbound flights from the West Coast regularly clock in under five hours and 30 minutes, while westbound sectors into winter headwinds can occasionally approach the six-hour mark.

According to a review published by Going, Delta Premium Select on domestic transcontinental routes provides a distinct upgrade over Delta Comfort+. With customized recline seats offering up to 38 in (97 cm) of pitch and 19 in (48 cm) of width, the cabin includes dedicated footrests, improved dining options, and priority boarding. Delta operates internationally configured widebodies, such as the Boeing 767-400ER and Airbus A330-900, on these premier domestic transcontinental routes, so travelers actually can get access to long-haul hardware on sub-six-hour sectors.

Delta Premium Select is sold on only select daily JFK–LAX rotations rather than every frequency, as noted by The Points Guy. The carrier mixes widebody operations with domestic narrowbodies that offer standard first class and Comfort+ seating instead. As North American carriers expand transcontinental premium offerings, the introduction of long-range narrowbodies like the Airbus A321XLR is set to make short-haul premium economy even more widespread.

Singapore Airlines removed economy from its longest flights but kept premium economy. The routes where premium seats matter may surprise you.

United Airlines has expanded its dedicated international Premium Plus cabin onto high-density domestic transcontinental routes connecting its primary hub network. On key sub-six-hour transcontinental corridors, such as Newark Liberty International Airport (EWR) to San Francisco International Airport (SFO) and Chicago O'Hare International Airport(ORD) to Los Angeles International Airport (LAX), United regularly schedules internationally configured widebodies, including 777-200ERs and 787-10s. Deploying these widebody aircraft on domestic hub-to-hub legs brings long-haul hardware directly to transcontinental travelers.

As detailed in routing and pricing analysis from Autopilot, United’s Premium Plus cabin features a 2-4-2 layout on widebodies, offering 38 in (97 cm) of seat pitch and 19 in (48 cm) of seat width. Passengers receive plush leather recline seats, footrests, individual power outlets, and upgraded inflight entertainment screens. On competitive transcontinental routes, United's Premium Plus tickets sit at a median price markup of $733 over standard economy, capturing high-yield corporate travelers whose companies restrict lie-flat business class bookings on sub-six-hour sectors.

United is further solidifying this domestic middle tier with the delivery of its A321XLR fleet. Configured with 12 dedicated Premium Plus seats in a 2-2 layout on narrowbody frames, the A321XLR allows United to offer consistent long-haul Premium Plus hardware on sub-six-hour transcontinental sectors without relying solely on widebody repositioning flights.

American Airlines is at the forefront of domestic transcontinental travel, deploying its purpose-built A321XLR fleet across premium sub-six-hour corridors. As detailed in operational analysis by Cirium, American has configured its A321XLR with a dedicated 12-seat premium economy cabin positioned between Flagship Suites and Main Cabin Extra. Scheduled on prime transcontinental routes such as New York (JFK) to San Francisco (SFO) and Boston (BOS) to Los Angeles (LAX), these five to six-hour sectors will see a move toward long-haul cabin hardware on single-aisle aircraft.

The 12-seat premium economy cabin on American’s A321XLR features a spacious 2-2 layout outfitted with Recaro seating platforms. Passengers enjoy 37 in (94 cm) of seat pitch, deep recline mechanisms, integrated leg and footrests, and 13.3-inch 4K touchscreen entertainment displays. Delivering true long-haul seating comfort, premium dining, and priority airport handling on sub-six-hour transcontinental flights, American provides a compelling middle-tier alternative for travelers seeking comfort without paying business class rates.

The introduction of dedicated premium economy cabins on short-haul and transcontinental sectors is equally being seen across the global aviation industry. Whether it be traveling on intra-Asia widebody routes or North American narrowbody transcontinental corridors, flyers no longer have to cross oceans to experience long-haul seating comfort. More and more airlines are now looking to expand custom-configured fleets like the A321XLR, so sub-six-hour premium economy will increasingly become the standard benchmark for regional premium flying.

Better Stock Split to Buy: Meta Platforms vs. Sandisk

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Sandisk and Meta's stock splits are being done in two different manners.

Meta Platforms (META +0.84%) and Sandisk (SNDK +3.55%) are each going through a stock split over the next few months. Sandisk is going through a traditional stock split, splitting its shares eight-for-one, effective Nov. 19. Meta's is a bit different. Instead of doing a traditional stock split of its existing stock, it's creating a new share class. This complicates things, but it's still going through a split at the end of the day.

So, which one makes the most sense to invest in? Let's take a look.

First, let's look at what's going on with Meta's stock. Its stock split is a bit goofy, but makes sense in the end. Overall, it's splitting its stock essentially two-for-one. It's doing this by creating a new Class C share. Class C shares will have the same value as Class A shares (the same ones any investor can buy on the market) initially, but in practice they will be worth slightly less over the long term.

The difference between Class C and Class A shares is that the new Class C shares have no voting power. This is important for Meta because, as it hands out Class C shares for acquisitions or employee compensation, CEO Mark Zuckerberg doesn't lose his voting control. This could be a red flag for some investors, but it's the direction that Meta is heading.

If you look at other stocks with split-share structures, like Alphabet (GOOG +0.78%) (GOOGL +0.78%), one share class trades at a discount to the other, but it's not a big enough price difference to be a big deal. So, if you're fine with the voting power of the company residing in the founder's hands, then this is nothing more than your run-of-the-mill two-for-one stock split. But if you're concerned about where Mark Zuckerberg is taking Meta, then it may be time to abandon ship.

For the remainder of the analysis, I'll assume that the average investor is OK with Zuckerberg's controlling power. But even with that, is it enough to be a better stock to own than Sandisk?

Sandisk and Meta aren't even close to being in the same industry. The closest relationship is Meta being a customer of Sandisk's, as it would buy solid-state drives (SSDs) from Sandisk for its data centers. Sandisk makes these devices, which have been in short supply due to the intensity of the artificial intelligence (AI) build-out. This has caused prices on these units to soar, and Sandisk has experienced huge growth as a result. During its latest quarter, revenue soared 378% year over year. That's simply incredible, and trumps anything Meta has delivered.

During Meta's Q3, its revenue rose 26% year over year to $64.6 billion. That's pretty fast among the big tech companies, but with how much Meta is spending on AI, investors want to see more. Still, this is a respectable result, and some of Meta's latest AI initiatives, such as Meta Muse, have received positive feedback and have seen heavy use. This could be a sign that Meta is headed in the right direction, and lead to better returns down the road.

Each stock is doing well in its industry, but there's still a huge piece of the puzzle missing: valuation. Sandisk is far cheaper than Meta, but that's because the market is concerned about a cyclical downturn in the industry once demand isn't as rampant.

With Sandisk growing rapidly and being priced so cheaply, I think it's the stock that I want to buy and hold right now. However, you'll have to keep a close eye on it to make sure that market conditions don't shift in the memory chip industry. If you're looking for a more set-it-and-forget-it type investment, then Meta makes more sense, as it's still in a great position to profit from the AI arms race if it continues to see success as it has in recent months.

Keithen Drury has positions in Alphabet and Meta Platforms. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet and Meta Platforms. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.
