Lyntris (LYNX) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Backlog doubled this year as international demand reshaped the growth outlook.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Lyntris Inc. (LYNX +28.66%) reported third quarter results characterized by significant revenue growth and a more than twofold increase in total backlog since the start of the fiscal year. Management reported that expansion is occurring across all mission domains, particularly within the Air & Missile Defense and Space ISR & Resilient Communications segments, which both saw triple-digit revenue growth. The company stated that its merchant supplier model allows it to support multiple prime contractors while maintaining high ownership of design intellectual property, which accounts for 85% of product revenue. Following its August IPO, the company has reduced its net leverage ratio to 1.2x and established a new $300 million credit facility to support future working capital needs and strategic initiatives.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] Good afternoon, and welcome to the Lyntris Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. [Operator Instructions] Please note that today's conference is being recorded. I will now turn the call over to Nathan Rutledge, Senior Vice Senior Vice President and Head of Investor Relations. Nathan, please go ahead.

Nathan Rutledge: Good afternoon, and welcome to Lyntris Third Quarter Fiscal Year 2026 Earnings Call. Please turn to Slide 2. Before we begin, please note that certain information presented on this call contains forward-looking statements. Forward-looking statements are based on current expectations, forecasts and assumptions that involve risks and uncertainties, including, but not limited to, economic, competitive, governmental, and technological factors outside of our control. These factors may cause our business strategy or actual results to differ materially from forward-looking statements.

For further information on these risks, please review the risk factors discussed in our registration statement on Form S-1 filed with the SEC as well as the Form 8-K filed today, the associated earnings release and the safe harbor statement contained therein. All forward-looking statements should be considered in conjunction with those in our earnings release. The company undertakes no obligation to revise any forward-looking statements in today's remarks or to update them to reflect events or circumstances occurring after this call. We will also discuss certain non-GAAP financial measures. Definitions and reconciliations to the most directly comparable GAAP measures are included in our earnings materials.

We have also posted a slide presentation at investors.lyntris.com in the News and Events section. The information discussed on this call is time-sensitive and is accurate only as of today, November 4, 2026. Joining me today are Brian Morrison, our Chief Executive Officer; Matt Alty, our President; and Tim Paulin, our Chief Financial Officer. With that, I'll turn the call over to you, Brian.

Brian Morrison: Thank you, Nathan, and welcome, everyone. I'm excited to share Lyntris' third quarter results. Our quarter was driven by strong performance, growing customer demand, and an expanding role on programs that matter to the future of defense and space. Let's look at Slide 4. I'll start with our third quarter highlights. Tim will review our financial results, and then Matt will discuss the capabilities and programs driving our growth. I'll then return for our outlook and priorities before we take your questions. In August, Lyntris made its debut on the New York Stock Exchange. That IPO strengthened our balance sheet and increased our capacity to invest in the business and support future growth.

Our focus now is to build on the strong business momentum we experienced leading up to the IPO and which we have continued to see since the listing. We'll continue to execute as these programs advance, translating today's opportunities into sustained long-term growth. Our purpose is straightforward. We help customers connect the modern battle space from sense to act. Our engineering talent and proprietary technology help detect threats, share and interpret information, and enable a response. We bring these capabilities together to meet demanding mission requirements, working with government agencies and military customers as well as both established and emerging primes.

Our track record of delivering for these customers positions us to grow as their programs advance and will take on a larger role as their needs evolve. In the third quarter, we delivered total revenue of $194 million, a year-over-year increase of 90% or 89% organically. Adjusted EBITDA grew 91% to $28.5 million, representing a margin of 14.7%. Adjusted net income was $17 million or $0.15 per share, and free cash flow for the quarter was $79 million. These results exceeded our expectations, demonstrating strong momentum in our business as we continue executing against our strategic priorities. Our third quarter performance reflects strong customer demand and the increasing relevance of our technology to evolving mission requirements.

Customers need to detect threats at greater distances, operate across more frequency bands, and respond with greater speed and accuracy. Those requirements closely suit the capabilities we brought together at Lyntris, from sensing and communications to software that connects information to command and control. We see broad-based growth across a number of programs, particularly in Air & Missile Defense and Space ISR & Resilient Comms, supported by the modernization of U.S. and allied radar systems and the growing need for resilient space-based communications. International customers are also investing in sovereign defense and space capabilities, creating additional opportunities for our technology.

Much of this increased activity supports well-funded high-priority programs with room to grow as programs move through the development life cycle. This work includes upfront engineering and initial production that generate revenue and adjusted EBITDA today but also establish the technology and manufacturing foundation for follow-on work. The first system requires more engineering effort, subsequent systems build on that work, creating the opportunity for higher margins as production expands. These program positions are an essential part of our long-term growth strategy. Engaging with customers early on allows us to embed our technology in their solutions and expand our role as programs mature. Our merchant supplier model also allows us to apply that technology across multiple customers and programs.

We welcome the additional revenue and adjusted EBITDA dollars from this work while continuing to focus on driving execution and profitability for the long term across the portfolio. That customer demand is also reflected in our backlog. We ended the quarter with a total backlog of just over $1 billion, up about $100 million sequentially and 113% compared with $476 million at the end of fiscal 2025. Our book-to-bill in the quarter was 1.5. Even with the strong revenue growth we delivered this quarter, orders continue to significantly exceed revenue, strengthening our visibility into future activity and supporting our confidence in the continued growth of this business.

Orders during the quarter included both initial awards and follow-on commitments that move programs from development into low rate production. International demand continues to support our growth. While international customers have historically represented about 15% of revenue, their share of our backlog has grown substantially in the fiscal year, accounting now for nearly 1/4 of our total backlog. These trends strengthen visibility into future activity and support our confidence in sustained growth. Slide 5 shows how that growth is taking shape across our core missions.

Air & Missile Defense revenue increased 154% to $83 million, reflecting accelerated activity on a major radar program where we are supporting initial fielding and low-rate initial production, along with contributions from programs across the production life cycle. Across this mission area, our positions span initial fielding, low rate production and programs preparing for higher production volumes. The absolutely essential modernization of U.S. and allied radar systems creates opportunities to build on these positions as customers demand greater range, accuracy, and sensitivity in response to a rapidly evolving threat environment. In Maritime Domain Awareness, revenue grew 29% to $54 million. Our work there spans naval radar, command and control, defensive cyber and integrated maritime sensor networks, including unmanned systems.

These capabilities help customers understand activity across their waters, protect mission networks, and coordinate a response. In Space ISR & Resilient Comms, revenue grew 108% to $57 million as we met rapidly growing sensing and communications demand. Here, we're expanding from integrated antenna solutions into full-up satellite payload solutions. Our recently announced role as a prime payload integrator on PTS-G illustrates that broader capability. We also see a substantial international opportunity in sovereign ground antenna and satellite payloads as allied nations seek resilient space-based communications capabilities tailored to their own requirements. Our space heritage across geostationary and medium earth orbits, together with our expertise in multi-frequency antennas gives us a strong foundation to address this emerging demand.

As requirements become more complex and deployment time lines compress, we see opportunities to bring these capabilities together into broader payload solutions and apply our technology across additional customer programs. Across all of our core mission areas, we are seeing opportunities to advance positions we've spent years establishing. Our engineering expertise and track record of delivery give us the foundation to support customers as their programs advance and requirements expand. This quarter demonstrated what that can mean for Lyntris. Customers asked us to do more, and we met the moment, driving growth in revenue and in our bottom line. Tim will now walk through the financial results in some greater detail.

Tim Paulin: Thank you, Brian. We're now on Slide 6. Our third quarter results reflect both growth in customer demand and faster pace of activity on existing programs. I'll walk through the financial detail on Slide 6, and then we'll cover free cash flow and our balance sheet. As Brian mentioned, revenue was $194 million, up 90% year-over-year or 89% organically, reflecting increased activity in Air & Missile Defense, integrated satellite payload programs and allied maritime programs. Adjusted EBITDA increased 91% to $28.5 million with a margin of 14.7%.

Higher revenue and cost actions offset the program mix, including more upfront engineering and initial production on programs where we are embedding our proprietary technology as well as growing investment in public company capabilities. As these programs move into higher rate production, we expect to benefit from the engineering work already completed and greater manufacturing scale. On a GAAP basis, we reported a net loss of $5 million or $0.04 per share compared to a net loss of $3.5 million or $0.03 per share in the prior year period. Adjusted net income was $17 million or $0.15 per share versus $1.7 million and $0.02 per share in the prior period.

Our adjusted net income measure excludes acquisitions and integration-related costs, stock-based compensation, amortization of acquired intangibles and certain other items together with related tax effects. The year-over-year improvement in adjusted net income was primarily driven by stronger results of operations, lower interest expense and adjustments for onetime items such as share-based compensation and loss on debt extinguishment. Turning to our results for the 9 months ended September 30, 2026. Revenue increased 55% to $435 million, reflecting stronger growth on new and existing programs. On a GAAP basis, we reported a net loss of $18 million or $0.17 per share compared to a net loss of $13.2 million or $0.14 per share in the prior year period.

Adjusted net income was $30.6 million or $0.28 per share versus $2.4 million and $0.03 per share in the prior period. The increase in our net loss is mainly attributable to nonrecurring stock-based compensation expenses and a loss on refinancing debt, partially offset by stronger operational performance and lower interest expense. Adjusted EBITDA grew 65% to $66.3 million, supported by top line growth and effective cost controls, yielding an adjusted EBITDA margin of 15.2%, a year-over-year increase of 90 basis points. Reconciliations of adjusted EBITDA, adjusted net income, adjusted diluted earnings per share and free cash flow to the corresponding GAAP measures are included in our earnings materials. Turning now to free cash flow.

Operating activities generated approximately $84 million of cash in the quarter, while capital expenditures were $5 million, resulting in a free cash flow of $79 million. Free cash flow excludes IPO proceeds. Through the first 9 months, operating cash flow was $119 million, capital expenditures were $11 million, core free cash flow of $108 million. Lyntris delivered strong free cash flow this quarter, supported by earnings and favorable customer payments. Across our portfolio, we received payments at different points in program execution, including upfront funding, progress payments and payments following delivery. We work with customers to align payment terms with program requirements and reduce the working capital required for growth.

Our mix of payment structures, disciplined working capital management, and low capital intensity support an attractive cash flow profile. Cash flow will vary from quarter-to-quarter as receipts and program spending move through the business. We expect continued strong cash generation as we execute our backlog, further strengthening our balance sheet and financial flexibility. We also took important steps to strengthen our balance sheet during the quarter. In July, we entered into a new $200 million term loan plus $100 million revolving credit facility, which came with materially lower interest rates than our prior facilities. Additionally, we used about $60 million of IPO proceeds to repay debt and ended the quarter with no balance on our revolver.

We ended the quarter with $88 million in cash and $198 million in debt, bringing net leverage to 1.2x, down significantly from 4.2x at the beginning of the fiscal year. Looking ahead, we expect net leverage to continue declining over time, although working capital requirements may lead to some quarter-over-quarter variability. Our strengthened balance sheet provides greater financial flexibility to support customer commitments, fund working capital needs, and invest in future growth. With that, I'll turn it over to Matt to discuss our capabilities and progress we're making across key programs. Matt?

Matthew Alty: Thank you, Tim. Let's now turn to Slide 7. I'd like to start by taking a step back and examining the mission environment because it explains why customers choose Lyntris. Warfare has changed in three fundamental ways. Firstly, our adversaries are adapting faster and the threats we encounter are more complex and numerous across every domain. We operate in a spectrum that is now congested, contested and weaponized. And detecting, tracking and responding to these threats require sensors with more range, sensitivity, and accuracy. Second, the data. Following the proliferation of sensor networks, data often remains siloed by service and platform, which is a problem when decision time lines are compressed.

Commanders need a clear integrated view of the battle space that enables faster, more informed decisions. Third, connectivity. Adversaries of industrialized electronic warfare and legacy architectures, satellites and waveforms can become single points of failure. The United States and its allies need secure, resilient links. And many allied nations are building sovereign capabilities to protect their own borders. Together, these dynamics are reshaping mission requirements and creating significant opportunities for Lyntris. Turning to Slide 8. Lyntris' capabilities cover the full path from sensing to action. Our sensors are used to detect threats and communicate data in highly contested environments. Our sensor architecture shapes how those sensors behave and prepares their output to be fused with other sources.

Our data and mission software then turns that information into an integrated picture that supports faster, more informed decisions. Our solutions are modular by design. We build proven hardware and software building blocks that can be adapted for different platforms and missions so a customer can start with a single component and scale to a fully integrated subsystem. That modularity is why our technology appears in every domain from integrated satellite payloads and into subsystems to radar on next-generation fighters, multiband ground terminals and maritime systems. The platforms differ, but the same core technology carries across each one.

Together, these capabilities support the 3 missions that Brian described earlier, Maritime Domain Awareness, Air & Missile Defense, and Space ISR & Resilient Communications. Slide 9 shows how we bring those capabilities to market. Our merchant vendor supplier model gives us multiple paths to participate in customer programs. We work with both established defense primes and newer entrants and our technologies often support more than one team competing for the same opportunity. We also work directly with end users, including the U.S. services and allied governments. Engaging early helps us understand their requirements and build our proprietary technology into the solutions that can advance with the program. Proprietary technology is central to that approach.

Approximately 85% of our product revenue comes from products built on design IP that we own. And the vast majority of our business comes from sole, single or directed source positions. As of the end of last year, we supported more than 200 programs. Those IP-derived positions provide continuity as programs advance and they are the foundation for the program momentum on the next slide. Let's turn to Slide 10, where 3 programs show how our capabilities address the needs we described earlier and how our role can grow over time. We'll begin with Air & Missile Defense.

LTAMDS is a program that we've supported from concept, and the program has recently transitioned into production and deployment phase under a U.S. Army contract valued at over $1.7 billion to the prime. In September, we announced production orders under a long-term purchase agreement with Raytheon, supporting demand through 2028. LTAMDS has been developed to provide the Army with 360-degree integrated Air & Missile Defense capabilities. The precision thermal management assemblies that we build at Lyntris help the radar's high-power electronics maintain stable temperatures and directly support the radar sensitivity, accuracy and range. Detecting and tracking the threat, however, is only the first step.

In Maritime Domain Awareness, our customers need to interpret data faster so they can make informed decisions, develop a plan and coordinate a response. Our command-and-control solutions bring information from multiple sensors into a shared operational picture, enabling faster, more informed decisions. For the Navy's Maritime Tactical Command and Control Program, or MTC2, we deliver a command and control platform that combines data integration, visualization and mission planning. It helps fleet planners at sea and at shore to understand the operating environment, plan missions, and coordinate operations. In Morocco, we adapted our Garda C2 technology to connect distributed sensors, operational sensors, mobile systems and ships, creating a common operating picture tailored to the Morocco Navy's requirements.

In Egypt, we are applying that approach to an awarded Maritime Domain Awareness program that brings together coastal sensors, operational centers, and command and control software. Each is an example of an established technology reaching another customer through an architecture tailored to its needs. In Space ISR & Resilient Communications, we're taking on a broader payload role. Earlier this month, we announced our selection as the prime payload integrator supporting the U.S. Space Force's Protected Tactical Satellite Communications Global Program or PTS-G. Our work spans radio frequency architecture, pointing systems, antenna and full payload integration and testing at our antenna center of excellence in Longmont, Colorado.

Building on 20 years of space heritage, this program expands our role from integrated antenna solutions to integrated satellite payloads. The same RF and antenna capabilities apply to sovereign payload opportunities that Brian described earlier. Many international customers need increasingly complex satellite systems that can operate in contested environments, often on compressed schedules. Building on established technology allows us to reduce technical risk and quickly adapt designs to their specific mission requirements. Together, these programs show how we leverage established technology, expand our role in mission-critical programs and create additional paths for growth. Slide 11 shows how those positions can develop through 4 stages: develop, deploy, expand, and sustain & upgrade.

The stages can overlap and we participate at different points across programs. In develop, we help establish the architecture and solve technical challenges on well-funded, high-priority customer programs. This upfront engineering work embeds our proprietary technology in the customer system and positions us for repeat production, incremental content, and margin expansion as the program scales. In deploy, we integrate and field the solution drawing on established designs, manufacturing processes and software. The LTAMDS program I shared a moment ago is a prime example where we are scaling production of thermal management technology to directly support a high-growth program. In expand, we bring that technology to additional customers or missions or take on more of an existing system.

Expansion can also mean leveraging the same proprietary technology to address a similar requirement on an adjacent program. For example, our thermal management IP supports multiple AE or radar programs and our RF and precision pointing technologies support multiple SATCOM programs, both domestically and internationally. Because we're a merchant supplier, we can support multiple customer teams at the same time. So an investment in our IP extends well beyond any single program award. Leveraging established technology and increasing production volumes can improve program economics over time.

In sustain & upgrade, we support fielded systems and add capabilities as requirements change or the system is upgraded to adapt to an emerging threat or emission requirement, including internationally, as we are doing with the Captor-E Mk2 radar upgrade for the U.K. Typhoon fleet. We support customers with programs at different stages of this life cycle. Programs entering production today often reflect years of earlier engineering work. At the same time, the development work we undertake today helps establish positions that can support growth for many years to come. Participating early allows us to help customers solve demanding technical requirements and incorporate our proprietary technology into the solution.

Successful execution then gives us a foundation to support subsequent deployments, production orders and upgrades. The mix of activity across these stages may affect margins from period to period. Our objective is to improve the profitability of established programs while building the next generation of growth opportunities. Back to Brian.

Brian Morrison: Thanks, Matt. That life cycle progression that Matt talked about is fundamental to our long-term growth strategy. We see opportunities to expand our role on existing programs, broaden the solutions we provide, and bring our technology to new customers and missions. Our priority is to translate those opportunities into sustained growth and strong financial performance. We're focused on aligning engineering, suppliers and production capacity with the commitments we already have in hand. We're also bringing more capabilities together around customer requirements. Our task now then is to turn product improvements into consistent execution and measurable financial results. Turning to Slide 12. I'll start with our fiscal 2026 outlook.

For full year 2026, we expect revenue of $623 million to $630 million, representing approximately 61% year-over-year growth at the midpoint. Adjusted EBITDA of $95.5 million to $97 million or expected growth of about 54%. We expect growth to come from increased activity on established Air & Missile Defense programs, continued integrated payload execution and delivery against already awarded maritime programs. Backlog conversion and follow-on orders support that opportunity with customer schedules and award timing influencing the pace of revenue. Our margin assumptions reflect higher volume, improved operating efficiency and a greater contribution from our technology, partly offset by continued initial fielding activity and investment to support growth.

With more design and initial deployment work than we had initially anticipated, we now expect adjusted EBITDA margin expansion to occur over a somewhat longer period than initially anticipated, although that work is helping us to overdrive EBITDA and revenue today. We remain confident in our long-term margin improvement strategy. Our work embeds our IP and customer solutions and creates opportunities for higher margin repeat production as programs expand. And as I suggested, we welcome the additional revenue and adjusted EBITDA dollars associated with this increased volume. We're building Lyntris around differentiated technologies, strong positions on critical programs and the ability to deliver integrated solutions across connected missions.

Our priorities are clear: execute consistently, expand our proprietary content, and drive greater efficiency as we scale. By doing so, we believe we can strengthen our competitive position, capture a greater share of our customers' evolving needs, and translate that growth into higher earnings and stronger cash generation over time. I want to thank our employees for their expertise and commitment, which helped us drive these results. I want to thank our customers for their trust and our shareholders for joining us on this next chapter. We look forward to updating you on our progress. With that, I'll take your questions.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] Our first question comes from the line of John Godyn with Citi.

John Godyn: The results are tremendous. To state the obvious, we've been in a market here where investors lack conviction and struggle to see through political risk that I suppose they think will derail growth across defense. So Brian and Matt, I just -- I wanted to use my question to dialogue a bit about that, what demand signals you're seeing on the ground that are driving this upside surprises to revenue? How much visibility you have that this momentum is going to continue? And hopefully, give us the conviction that the market right now is lacking.

Brian Morrison: Yes. Thanks, John. I appreciate the question, and I'm happy to have you here with us today. I would say the business is oriented, and I think you've heard me say this before, around those missions that we believe must endure no matter what. Those national and international security priorities that have bipartisan support among national security thinkers in really meaningful and long-lasting ways. There are just some inescapable truths about our national security situation today. We are not where we should be in terms of our ability to detect and respond to, for example, air and missile threats.

And regardless of what the political landscape or the market landscape looks like on any given day, this is a meaningful problem that the U.S. government has to solve. And it's a problem that is understood by policymakers inside the executive branch and the legislative branch across parties. Matt, do you want to add anything to that?

Matthew Alty: Yes. I think in addition to what Brian just said, I think if you look at our core technology, John, whether it's operating over more frequency bands or operating over more power, we're seeing that applicability increase. So that's really driving additional growth. And we're seeing that not only here in the U.S., but we talked about this previously. But when you look at it internationally, that desire to have European nations to have their own sovereign capabilities, particularly in space, particularly when they're operating now in contested environments where you've got to have resilient communication or data transmission plays directly into that core IP that we've developed over the past few years.

And that's a key driver that we see continuing for the near-term.

Brian Morrison: Now you also asked about visibility, John, and a 1.5 book-to-bill in a discrete quarter when you have a record-breaking revenue is a pretty good indicator, I think, of both the durability of our demand and our visibility going forward as a backlog over $1 billion.

John Godyn: Excellent. And if I could just follow-up. At the end of the prepared remarks, there was some commentary about the margin outlook going forward. Can we just maybe take a second to elaborate on that and just kind of understand how you guys see the margins from here?

Brian Morrison: Yes. In short, I continue to believe that in the medium and long term, this is a company with a two handle on our margin rate. The question is how rapidly will we get there. And I think that we have talked about 100 bps a year of margin improvement. I still think that's achievable. But as you saw in this quarter, we had some modest margin compression, although certainly growth in EBITDA and top line. So we're just growing from a different point. And as I said, it will take perhaps a somewhat longer period to get there, but I continue to believe this is a two-handle business.

Operator: Our next question comes from the line of Louie DiPalma with William Blair.

Louie Dipalma: Great. Brian, Matt, Tim, and Nathan, congrats on your first earnings as a public company and the results. For my first question, there has been significant industry investment and a lot of excitement associated with LEO satellite communications. And you have won several contracts related to GEO and NEO satellites. You discussed the PTS-G, and I believe you've also won work for some international customers, I believe, in Europe. Can you discuss why you expect GEO will continue to play a major role on the battlefield operations? And what of your IP is really a key technology here that allows you to provide these like anti-jammable radio communications for satellites.

Matthew Alty: Yes. Thanks for the question, Louie. I'll kind of provide a couple of parts to my answer. I mean, the core of everything we do from a space perspective is our heritage. We've got over 20 years of space heritage, specifically in the GEO and the NEO orbits. As you know, those are very, very challenging orbits. You're typically sealing an asset that's of a high value that has to operate for a number of years and continue to perform as it did the day that it was launched. And that heritage really gives us a seat at the table when it comes to meeting these very, very challenging mission requirements.

To the resiliency part of your question as to why we're uniquely positioned to meet those requirements, our vertically integrated capability where we can design, build and test gives us a unique position where we can work with our customers very, very early as they're looking to meet those mission requirements and develop and deploy solutions far faster than kind of other suppliers in the industry. And that's given us an opportunity to win not only those GEO and NEO opportunities here in the U.S., but we're seeing a lot of demand right now from European nations who have woken up to the fact that they don't have guaranteed access to U.S. assets.

If you look at their requirements specifically over in Europe, they're looking for assured communications that can be fielded in a contested environment and provide that connectivity regardless of that operating environment. We've got some very, very specific antenna IP that allows us to build complex payloads that meet those very, very specific mission requirements. And obviously, in GEO, you can position one satellite to provide coverage in a very, very specific geographic region, whereas with LEO, you need these broader constellations. Just one final comment that I would add is we do support LEO missions as well.

But when you think about that initial question around where do we see growth kind of over the next 5 to 10 years, we see significant demand across those more complex difficult missions in GEO and LEO, where we have some very unique IP that allows us to differentiate ourselves versus, say, the more commoditized LEO markets.

Louie Dipalma: And for Tim, as it relates to the margin trajectory, have you been winning a significant number of research and development contracts that perhaps have a lower margin initially and then the margin is just going to scale up as the projects move to like low rate initial production and then full rate initial production? Or just as a high level, can you explain like the lumpiness in the margin?

Tim Paulin: I think you captured the concept correctly, although I would adjust your initial research and development comment. It's not research and development. It is design and development, I would say. So they are earlier stage programs that we've -- there was an outsized proportion of those types of programs in the quarter, which is affecting the margin the way you're commenting. But yes, your second part of your comment, I would strongly agree with as those programs go to low rate, full rate, we expect to see margin expansion there.

Brian Morrison: Yes. Louie, Brian here. You've hit on a core feature of the business, though, which is that we do work in the early stages of a program life cycle because that gives us access and sticky access to the latter stages of a program life cycle where we have opportunities to add ever more of our content and do ever more for our customers and frankly, just generate operating efficiencies in the manufacturing world. So we see those margins just naturally on many of those programs improving over time.

Operator: Your next question comes from the line of Ronald Epstein with Bank of America.

So how are you guys thinking about that?

Brian Morrison: Yes. So I agree with you that I am comfortable with our leverage position where it is. And I'll note, of course, that we're growing and naturally delevering. Now as to capital allocation, you're right to note that there's cash on the balance sheet. And the way Matt and I think about capital allocation is roughly as follows: One, we have a disciplined strategic view of technologies that we're interested in and technology areas where we want to be. So we will deploy capital to access those technology nodes either through M&A or through IRAD. Now that said, those are, I think, the principal ways in which we would deploy capital.

I think it's also possible that we would do some opportunistic share repurchase, where we think that we are undervalued in the market. If we have cash, we might do some share repurchase in the future after running the appropriate regulatory traps for that. I don't think that there is a possibility that we would use that excess cash to delever because we're already delevering from growth and I don't anticipate that we would use that cash for a dividend. But aside from those exceptions, I think we're just always looking for the highest and best use of our precious cash and our shareholder capital. Matt, do you want to add anything to that?

Matthew Alty: I think you covered it, right.

Andrew Steinhardt: Got you. That's very helpful. And I guess -- just a quick follow-up. Can you talk about the Minnesota Tech hub? What sort of products will actually be developed there and why St. Paul?

Brian Morrison: Yes. So we recognized that location as a great source of talent, particularly in some emerging work we're doing in both health care and in radars. The health care part of the business is a small part of the business, but it's important. It's about cutting-edge solutions for military health care going forward. We're also looking to put some of our radar work into that location. It's just a great source of talent, and we've had really good relationships with the Minnesota community, and that's sort of what led us there.

Matthew Alty: Yes. I don't want to get into the specifics of management reserve. A great example would be one of our recent acquisitions where we acquired a business that brought us motion control technology for some of the antenna that we would use in our space applications.

The investment into that technology will be directly applicable to a whole range of resilient solutions that we'll field either in space or on ground applications. So for sure, as we take that IP, invest into it now and then apply it to future wins, we're very, very confident we'll see margin growth as we do that.

Brian Morrison: Yes. I think Matt's right. I think we think of that space payload business, that space payload part of the business as a franchise. And as we are developing and designing, testing technologies for today's customer, they will be applicable to the payload pipeline that we have that is robust that will develop -- that will result in better margins going forward on that future work. That is not to say that we're not always looking at our management reserves and our EACs on any given project as it goes forward to get margin growth, but that just gives you a sense of how we think about it.

Matthew Alty: Yes. I would just kind of add to Brian's point there. I would think of this more in terms of nonrecurring engineering NREs that we are thinking through as we kind of bid that work. There was not a meaningful change in what we think of as our weighted average period of performance. So I think backlog comparisons here are fair. There's no sort of 7-year award that has dropped into the backlog and affected it in a meaningful way. So then I think the first part of your question was what are the real levers to driving margin rates higher?

And I think they're as follows: One, we have talked, I think, somewhat less on this call than I might have anticipated about our really rapidly growing international business, which is somewhat more accretive from a margin perspective. It's also true that we're signing ever more firm fixed price work, which although it carries some modest amount of increased risk, it also carries the reward of higher margin rates. As our revenue is growing at this high rate, of course, we see operational leverage as we move forward, and we'll look for that to drive our margin rates going forward.

And then finally, we are really at an inflection point, and you see it first in our space business, but there's other places where I think we'll soon be seeing it, where we're really recognizing the power and capability of the entire company on a job-over-job basis. And what that means is that we're able to do more and more for the customer, and that work generates a higher margin because it's higher value for the customer. Matthew Alty: I would just build upon that last comment you made. I mean, our space payload is a great example of that, where we've moved from individual antenna solutions to now integrated antennas that kind of are built for a very, very specific mission requirement. And now we're taking that and building that into an integrated payload. That obviously comes with both revenue and margin benefit as we move up that value chain to a higher-level assembly.

Rams listed Matthew Stafford with elbow injury because reporters asked about ice on it

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

On Monday, reporters asked Rams coach Sean McVay about quarterback Matthew Stafford having ice on his throwing arm after Sunday’s win over the Chargers. On Wednesday, the Rams listed Stafford as having an elbow injury.

“Really, it’s because you guys asked me about it earlier in the week,” McVay told reporters on Friday regarding the decision to disclose an elbow injury for Stafford. “It’s more semantical than anything else. It’s normal, like what he told you guys, but because you guys asked me about him having ice on his elbow. . . . He’s been full, hasn’t missed any sort of reps so normal management stuff that he’s been doing over the last few years.”

It conjures memories of the Patriots’ one-time habit of listing former quarterback Tom Brady as “probable” with a “right shoulder” injury. That all started because he had ice on his shoulder in the locker room, reporters inquired, and the team decided to just put him on the injury report every week.

Has the elbow situation affected Stafford’s throwing?

“I haven’t seen any of that,” McVay said. “I told you guys he’d be better equipped to talk about it. He’s been a full participant, been awesome. He’s spinning the ball. He’s been able to activate all parts of the field with great timing, rhythm, and accuracy so he’s been great.”

So either he has an actual elbow injury that the Rams had been concealing, or he doesn’t really have an elbow injury but they listed him as having one because reporters noticed ice on his arm and asked McVay about it. Neither explanation seems to reflect the true spirit of the injury report, which expects real injuries — and only real injuries — to be disclosed.

Either Stafford has an elbow injury that was previously hidden or he doesn’t have an elbow injury and the claim that he has one is a middle finger to the meddling reporters who were doing their jobs. Neither explanation is a preferred alternative to the unvarnished truth.

Adam Driver Won’t Return for ‘Girls’: ‘I Wish Them the Best of Luck’

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Adam Driver had a major breakout thanks to his role on “Girls,” but the actor shut down the idea of returning for the reboot of the iconic series in a recent interview.

While talking about his role in James Gray’s new movie “Paper Tiger,” Driver was asked by Cinema Blend if he will be returning for the revival. “No, but I wish them the best of luck,” he told the outlet.

News of the new “Girls” series broke in mid-October, with creator and star Lena Dunham set to return as Hannah Horvath alongside co-stars Allison Williams as Marnie Michaels, Jemima Kirke as Jessa Johansson and Zosia Mamet as Shoshanna Shapiro. Andrew Rannells also returns to the role of Elijah Krantz. Related Stories ‘Wildwood’ Director Travis Knight on AI: 'An Amazing Piece of Technology’ but ‘I’m Not Embracing It at All’ ‘The Mosquito Bowl’ Star Tom Francis on Singing in the Movie Last Minute and the ‘Visceral and Intense’ World War II Scenes

The series will have a limited five-episode run on HBO with production starting in 2027.

Driver’s career took off after his run as Hannah’s boyfriend on “Girls.” In addition to his role as Kylo Ren in three “Star Wars” movies, he starred in “Marriage Story” and “The Meyerowitz Stories” for Noah Baumbach; “The Last Duel” and “House of Gucci” for Ridley Scott; “Ferrari” for Michael Mann and this year’s “Misty Green.” He’s also worked with director Jim Jarmusch on “Paterson,” “The Dead Don’t Die” and “Father Mother Sister Brother.”

In her recent memoir “Famesick,” Dunham described her tumultuous relationship with Driver, saying, “He could be short-tempered and verbally aggressive, condescending and physically imposing. He could also be protective, loving even,” Dunham writes. After the last episode of “Girls” wrapped, Dunham says Driver told her, “I hope you know I’ll always love you” before he said goodbye.

XRP Price Trapped at $1.1799 After $30 Million Cascade Liquidation

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

XRP’s leveraged market went through a series of brutal flushes this week. In just five days, derivative traders who bet on the token’s upside have lost more than $30 million, according to CoinGlass. What happened is a sudden price reversal after a moment of optimism on November 4 that caught buyers off guard and set off a cascade of liquidations all across the crypto board.

Liquidations hit extreme levels by Friday, running 2.74 times above the weekly average. Exchanges' data shows a completely lopsided picture where longs account for 95.54% of all daily losses worth $16.01 million out of a total $16.75 million. Shorts got off with a scare, losing a token $746,000. Of course, Binance as the most liquid platform was the place of the most pain, with $8.06 million in leveraged positions on XRP wiped out on the exchange.

What played out is a textbook long squeeze. Traders opened overleveraged positions hoping the rally would continue, got trapped in a falling trend, and their forced closures started knocking each other out down the chain. More than 3,600 traders worldwide lost their deposits completely.

XRP dropped 6.41% over the past days on the spot market, leaving the price pinned at $1.1799 by the weekend. How strong sellers are is evident by the XRP/USD chart on TradingView. Here the weekly candle comes to closure with a long upper wick, meaning every bullish attempt to take control and buy the dip above $1.22 was shut down.

For XRP buyers, the technical picture looks worrying:

The price has settled below key moving averages, the 50 EMA and 100 EMA. The latter has now turned into hard resistance at $1.271.

The Relative Strength Indicator is at 33.21 with the 'bearish' divergence appearing earlier on the chart fully playing out, and the trend is clearly pointing down.

The next dangerous trigger for the market is at $1.1588 as that’s where the nearest “long pain” zone lies, according to Coinglass. The price is just 1.79% away from that line.

Algorithms put the odds of the chart testing this level within the next 24 hours at roughly 50%. If bulls give up this line too, a liquidation cascade will force-close another 928,900 in positions and speed up the decline. Buyers right now have no support above current XRP price, while sell pressure below is estimated at under half a million of dollars.

The 8 Best Castlevania Games Of All Time, Ranked

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Castlevania is one of the most beloved franchises in video game history, despite a period of 13 years when we didn’t see a new installment until the launch of Castlevania: Belmont’s Curse earlier this year.

Over the years, the games have provided a variety of experiences, mixed with rich lore built on the vampire myth. With so many games available to choose from, it can be difficult to know where to begin if you haven’t touched the series in a while. Accordingly, here is our ranked list of the 8 best Castlevania games. It should make an excellent starting point.

When Castlevania came to the Super Nintendo, it needed to impress players who were still deciding whether they wanted to spend money on a new generation of Nintendo hardware. Fortunately for everyone involved, the team at Konami was up to the task. Super Castlevania 4 features beautiful environments, as well as a hero who can now whip in eight directions, even while jumping. It’s also possible to grapple to distant points, or to wield the whip as a shield. Early use of Mode 7 rotation provided some visual showpieces to go along with parallax depth that brought static environments to life. The adventure starts outside the castle, allowing Simon to explore its harsh exterior before venturing inside for a final showdown with the lord of the manor. If you’re looking for a starting point, you could do much worse, and it’s conveniently available as part of the Castlevania Anniversary Collection on Switch, PlayStation 4, PC, and Xbox One.

The Super Nintendo wasn’t the only hardware to feature a high-quality Castlevania game during the 16-bit era. Castlevania: Bloodlines took a unique approach, introducing a hero who strikes with a spear. He can use it to access alternate routes that the other character (who fights with a more traditional whip) cannot. The pacing was fast and furious, in keeping with many Genesis games of that time. The setting was also more modern than the other classic titles, taking place during the first World War. Stages are inspired by locations around the globe, including a visit to the Leaning Tower of Pisa, among other attractions. Throw in some stunning visual tricks that made full use of the Genesis hardware, along with a beautiful score from composer Michiru Yamane (who would go on to compose for Symphony of the Night) and you have a truly special entry in the franchise.

Originally developed for the Sharp X68000 and released on that hardware as Akumajo Dracula, Castlevania Chronicles was one of the final Castlevania games built in the classical style. It actually is a remake of 1986’s first entry in the series. 1993’s version offered greatly improved visuals and sound, while providing generally fair but challenging gameplay that is some of the most difficult the series ever offered. Some new twists maintain the element of surprise, even as players retread familiar ground, but there’s no better way to experience the original adventure, as its developers intended. When I checked earlier this year, the digital version of the prettied up PlayStation port was still available to purchase through the PlayStation Store on a PlayStation 3 console, if you add the funds to your wallet on newer hardware. Or you can hunt down a physical copy of that port, which should play just fine on your PlayStation 3.

If more people had easily been able to play the game as its developers intended, Castlevania: Rondo of Blood might be remembered today as the premier Castlevania experience. Sadly, the game remained exclusive to the PC Engine CD hardware for a long time. It didn’t head to North America and Europe until it arrived on PSP as part of 2007’s Castlevania: The Dracula X Chronicles collection, and as a Virtual Console selection in 2010, almost a decade and a half after its initial release. The closest we got in the meantime was Dracula X on the Super Nintendo. That title was a mostly unsuccessful attempt to compress Rondo of Blood’s essence to fit on a cartridge — and to reach hardware that hadn’t bombed outside of Japan. The same plot and characters returned, as did many enemy sprites. Both games began in a town on fire. However, the branching stages were absent, replaced by abbreviated challenges that players found frustrating. Some characters from the original version weren’t even playable, and the sound quality suffered immensely. Rondo of Blood is an important game in Castlevania’s timeline, and concludes with the events that serve as a prologue to Symphony of the Night. If you haven’t yet done so, you should play through it soon.

Castlevania: Aria of Sorrow followed one year after Harmony of Dissonance, which in some respects was the more spectacular of the pair. But despite its inferior visuals, Aria of Sorrow offered the richer experience on a mechanical level. Players control Soma Cruz, who blacks out during the first solar eclipse of the century and awakens in Dracula’s castle. There, he finds he can absorb the essence of the monsters he vanquishes, a useful way to gain the strength he’ll need to reach Dracula’s throne room and rescue his classmate. The adventure is surprisingly complex, given the tiny hardware that houses it, and still a lot of fun to play. You can most easily experience it and other games of that era as part of Castlevania Advance Collection on Switch, PlayStation 4, PC, and Xbox One.

A weekly cheat sheet to what you’ll eventually love to play. No buying, no commitment — just saving for later.

The 8-bit era established Castlevania as a force to be reckoned with, ensuring it survived long enough to continue breaking new ground over the decades. But those early titles aren’t always a lot of fun to play today. The original game sometimes felt cheap and rather bland, even near release. Its sequel had an interesting plot and ambitious new mechanics, but it confounded many players with unclear, non-linear objectives. Then Castlevania 3: Dracula’s Curse came around and perfected the formula, providing multiple heroes with lore (which Netflix later explored with an animated series) and unique play styles, along with diverging paths through gorgeous stages that pushed the NES hardware harder than ever before. It’s the finest single example of what Castlevania could be during that era, and well worth revisiting today.

Check our full Castlevania: Belmont’s Curse review for all the details, but the short version is this: you need to play the game. It respects the series’ history while mapping out a potential future we hope the developers will one day realize with follow-up titles. The 2.5D visuals give each area a new sense of depth, while enabling the satisfying Metroidvania mechanics players have enjoyed since Symphony of the Night changed the franchise forever. Fluid movement during combat, a killer soundtrack to match the moonlit ambience of the visuals, and a brave new hero all add up to one of the very finest entries the series has ever seen.

Nearly 30 years after its release, there’s still no topping Castlevania: Symphony of the Night. It’s the game credited with taking Metroid’s design and fusing it with the gothic adventuring players associate with the Castlevania series. The sprawling castle was populated by nightmarish creatures, many of them familiar and many of them not. Stripped of his powerful weapons, Alucard explores the castle and seeks to put down his fearsome father once and for all. But as players learned, there’s much more to the adventure than the simple castle they glimpse beneath the moonlight. One of the happiest surprises in video games came when players looked at things through a new perspective, thanks to some holy spectacles. The shift in focus opened up an unfamiliar pathway, revealing that they weren’t even halfway done with their adventure. It was the game that kept on giving, and it still hasn’t stopped.

Hoffenheim vs Schalke 04 - prediction, team news, lineups

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

By Anthony Nolan | 07 Nov 2026 03:51, Last updated: 07 Nov 2026 04:22

Relegation-threatened Schalke 04 will travel to SNP Arena on Sunday, hoping to catch floundering Bundesliga opponents Hoffenheim off guard before the international break.

Die Kraichgauer are looking for a first win in four games, while Die Knappen are desperate to avoid a fifth defeat in six matches.

Christian Ilzer's Hoffenheim were surprise contenders for Champions League football last season, and though they fell agonisingly short by finishing fifth, the club were aiming to build on that success.

However, Die Kraichgauer have endured a campaign of fluctuations, kicking off this term by losing four of their first five Bundesliga clashes before recording a trio of successive victories in the middle of last month.

Unfortunately for Ilzer, the pendulum of his team's form has swung back towards the negative once again, as the hosts prepare for this contest having failed to come out on top in any of their last three matches across all competitions, losing twice.

On Thursday, Hoffenheim suffered their latest frustration when they conceded a 91st-minute equaliser in a 1-1 Europa League draw with Crystal Palace, who had been reduced to 10 men when Adam Wharton was shown a second yellow card.

In the two games prior, Die Kraichgauer were eliminated from the DFB-Pokal in a penalty shootout against second-tier Holstein Kiel, before a 2-0 defeat at Werder Bremen last weekend.

That Halloween loss at the Weserstadion has left Ilzer's men 13th in the table with nine points, a tally that hardly provides security considering the club are sitting just two points above 16th-placed Union Berlin and three above their opponents in the automatic relegation spots.

Meanwhile, Miron Muslic's Schalke lifted the 2.Bundesliga title last term en route to their top-flight return, but they have fared the worst of any of the three promoted teams.

Die Knappen are 17th with only six points to their name after eight matches, and unless they avoid defeat, the club could find themselves at the bottom of the table for the international break, given they are just two points ahead of a rejuvenated Borussia Monchengladbach.

That fear could become reality on Sunday, with Muslic's side arriving in Sinsheim having lost four of their past five games across all competitions, including a 2-0 defeat to RB Leipzig last Saturday.

To make matters worse, the visitors are the lowest scorers in the entirety of the Bundesliga with four goals, and have blanked in each of their four most recent outings.

Schalke head to SNP Arena as significant underdogs and with little in the way of a silver lining to inspire hope, but Hoffenheim have been far from perfect this season, and Die Knappen know that a surprise win would likely lift them out of the bottom two, with Union set to face Freiburg.

Hoffenheim manager Ilzer made it clear in his press conference that the team have a number of players whose readiness will be examined closer to kick off, including right-back Vladimir Coufal (thigh) and versatile attacker Adam Hlozek (muscle).

The hosts are missing a slew of defenders, though, with Albian Hajdari (plantar fascia), Robin Hranac (meniscus) and Valentin Gendrey (fractured fibula) all unavailable, so Koki Machida and Ozan Kabak could start at the heart of Ilzer's backline, flanked by Bernardo and 18-year-old Natnael Abraha.

Midfielders Bambase Conte and Nathan De Cat are also sidelined due to respective adductor and meniscus injuries, though Wouter Burger and Leon Avdullahu should be on hand to start in the centre of the park, platforming Fisnik Asllani in the number 10 role.

Further forward, winger Patrick Wimmer is dealing with a muscle injury, but Alexander Prass and Adam Daghim will be ready to support striker Tim Lemperle up top.

As for Schalke, they are also missing defenders, with right-back Adrian Gantenbein (muscle) and centre-back Maximilian Wober (thigh) out, and the duo of Nikola Katic and Hasan Kurucay are expected to be joined by Timo and Vitalie Becker in their absence.

Janik Bachmann and fellow midfielder Ron Schallenberg are also dealing with hip and leg injuries, leaving Satoshi Tanaka and Soufiane El-Faouzi to start behind Kenan Karaman.

Elsewhere, striker Moussa Sylla could return from his left leg problem after the international break, but veteran Edin Dzeko looks set to lead the line on Sunday.

Baumann; Abraha, Machida, Kabak, Bernardo; Burger, Avdullahu; Prass, Asllani, Daghim; Lemperle

Karius; T Becker, Katic, Kurucay, V Becker; Tanaka, El-Faouzi; Ljubicic, Karaman, Aouchiche; Dzeko

Hoffenheim have struggled once again in recent weeks, but they will be quietly confident about their chances of beating one of the division's relegation favourites on Sunday.

Schalke's troubles in front of goal are well-documented, and it would be a surprise to see them find the back of the net, never mind walk away with anything from this clash.

Forget ‘Twin Peaks,’ Ben Affleck’s Forgotten 7-Episode Mystery Was Ahead of Its Time

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Amanda M. Castro is a Network TV writer at Collider and a New York–based journalist whose work has appeared in Newsweek, where she is an Associate Editor, and The U.S. Sun, where she previously served as a Senior Consumer Reporter.

She specializes in network television coverage, delivering sharp, thoughtful analysis of long-running procedural hits and ambitious new dramas across broadcast TV. At Collider, Amanda explores character arcs, storytelling trends, and the cultural impact of network series that keep audiences tuning in week after week.

Born and raised in Puerto Rico, Amanda is bilingual and holds a degree in Communication, Film, and Media Studies from the University of New Haven.

Ben Affleck is having quite the moment on Netflix. His latest thriller, Animals, has held the No. 1 spot on the streamer's global English-language movie chart for four consecutive weeks, pulling in another 4.7 million views and 8.4 million hours watched between October 26 and November 1, 2026. But long before his latest streaming success, Affleck tried his hand at something completely different: creating one of network television's strangest mystery series.

In 2002, Affleck and his producing partner Sean Bailey co-created Push, Nevada, a surreal mystery about a mild-mannered IRS agent who stumbles into a desert town filled with corruption, cryptic clues, and slow-dancing strangers. It sounded like Twin Peaks meets The X-Files — until ABC added one wild twist: Viewers could win a million dollars by solving the mystery themselves. The result was a show too ambitious, too strange, and too ahead of its time to survive more than seven episodes.

Push, Nevada followed Jim Prufrock (Derek Cecil), an accountant who receives a mysterious fax suggesting embezzlement at the Versailles Casino. When he heads to Push — a nowhere town drenched in neon and paranoia — he discovers that its residents haven’t filed a tax return since 1985 and that an ominous company, Watermark LLC, seems to control everything from the police to the local dive bar. Oddballs populated the noir-ish mystery: Scarlett Chorvat’s Mary, a “Slo-dancer” warning Prufrock to get out while he still can; Jon Polito’s corrupt casino boss; and Armand Assante’s slick criminal known only as “Mr. Smooth.”

Four Netflix characters, four completely different ways of showing up for somebody. This is the one who fits how you actually do friendship, and what having them in your life would be like.

You do not do friendship at half volume, and neither does he. What you want is somebody who says yes to the plan before you have finished describing the plan.

You are the friend who makes things happen, and so is she. Together you would be mildly exhausting to everyone around you and completely delighted with yourselves.

You have no patience for pleasant lies, and she has no ability to tell them. What looks like coldness from the outside is the most reliable friendship on this list.

You look after whoever is having the worst day. So does he. It is the least strategic way to move through the world and the best possible quality in a friend.

It looked incredible, with cinematography by Jim Denault, known for Boys Don’t Cry, and a moody palette of reds, golds, and shadows. Critics compared its tone and aesthetic directly to David Lynch’s Twin Peaks — from the quirky characters to the eerie score and slightly stilted dialogue. But while Lynch’s small-town surrealism invited obsession, Push, Nevada asked for something even stranger: participation.

Each episode contained hidden clues — web addresses, code words, even on-screen numbers — leading viewers closer to a real-life prize of $1,045,000. Fans could follow the story across fake websites, emails, and even physical mailers in what producers dubbed “the three-ball theory” of engagement: viewers could wade in, swim, or dive deep. The experiment made Push, Nevada one of the earliest examples of transmedia storytelling on network TV.

Unfortunately, it also made it unwatchable for casual audiences. The mystery was dense, the tone self-serious, and the clues often so obvious (highlighted in red boxes during reruns) that the whole thing felt like homework. Competing against CSI and Will & Grace, Push, Nevada was canceled after seven episodes — though ABC still had to finish the contest legally. The winner, New Jersey native Mark Nakamoto, solved the final puzzle and claimed the million-dollar prize, but the show itself vanished into cult TV history.

The longtime friends have something bigger in mind for their next chapter.

Today, Push, Nevada feels like a bizarre artifact from the early 2000s — part prestige mystery, part ARG, part marketing stunt. However, despite its flaws, it accurately predicted how audiences would one day crave deeper, more interactive storytelling through shows, websites, and games.

A 100-Foot Fall Stole Her Mobility. Now, Former Climbing Ranger Quinn Brett Is Rebranding Trails for Everyone.

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Quinn Brett photographed in Chasm Meadows in Rocky Mountain National Park, Colorado (Photo: Courtesy of Brian Watson/Jacob Bailey)

In 2017, National Park Service (NPS) climbing ranger Quinn Brett, 36, fell while ascending El Capitan in Yosemite National Park. The 120-foot drop left her paralyzed from the waist down. In a moment, she went from climbing soaring rock faces to moving in a wheelchair.

Like many Americans with mobility disabilities, Brett faced challenges when she returned to outdoor places like parklands, trail systems, and even national parks. Some of America’s wildest places were no longer accessible to her.

“To become disabled, and realize that I can’t even get across the street because there isn’t a curb, for instance, was quite a change in perspective,” she told Outside.

That’s why Brett founded the accessibility consulting firm Dovetail Trail Consulting to make outdoor spaces more accessible and usable for everyone. With her help, the Bureau of Land Management (BLM) launched a new interactive accessibility map on October 13, connecting visitors with trails, bathrooms, and campsites that are accessible to people with mobility disabilities.

“People with disabilities don’t want to just go to the one-mile paved trail in the busiest parts of our national parks,” Brett said.

As part of the BLM map, visitors can snap photos of public lands and submit them, along with detailed descriptions of the infrastructure. Other users can then better understand accessibility before they hit the trail.

“You don’t have to do a construction project,” Brett said. “Just take pictures.”

Nearly one in ten Americans have mobility issues, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. For these people, Brett says enjoying the outdoors is not just about finding the right device, but also finding trails that are made for wheels.

“For a long time we only had one pair of shoes to pick, but now with mobility device technology, we have a whole bunch of types of shoes that we get to choose from, and we want to go use our shoes in a variety of places,” Brett said.

Kevin Oliver, chief of the BLM Division for Recreation and Business Services, participated in the three-year map project. He told Outside that the goal is to help people plan their trip and avoid surprises from site conditions.

“We’re going to continue to work to deliver more information and better information to the recreation public, so that all types of interests can be served,” Oliver told Outside. “The BLM recognizes the work is never really done. You know, we’re always perfecting it.”

The map includes 50 existing accessible recreation opportunities. One such example is McCoy Flats in Utah, a map entry that shows the parking lot’s packed gravel, extended picnic tables, and the entrances to vault toilets.

The tool also identifies 74 additional sites where the BLM has proposed improvements. Some, like Colorado’s Blue Valley Ranch Trail, are fully funded and in progress. Others, such as New Mexico’s Glade Run Adaptive Trail, are still hunting for funding.

BLM’s new map is one piece of a broader push to expand trail access across federal agencies.

Gathering the information is only the beginning. Julia Ray, chief operating officer of the adaptability nonprofit Move United, told Outside that keeping information up to date matters just as much.

“Trails are kind of living things,” Ray said. “They change with weather and seasons, so it has to be an ongoing process to keep that information current, and where and how that information is shared is still inconsistent.”

Move United partnered with Mount Rainier and Grand Canyon National Parks to assess more than 17 miles of trails for accessibility. The organization also created a free trail-access course to help local land managers understand federal accessibility standards.

Improving access at popular trails is a great way to let more people share in the national park experience, Ray said.

“If the public go to a trail more than others, that’s probably where anybody else would want to go as well,” she said. “Everybody deserves the right to access the outdoors, and we are just really excited that there’s some energy around this right now.”

For trail users like Brett, having accessibility details means she can spend less time guessing and more time choosing where to go, what equipment to bring, and how much challenge she wants to take on. Her desire to be in the mountains hasn’t gone away, and as she says, it never will.

“I still want to be playing and going to the top of mountains and doing wild and crazy things,” Brett says. “But I also need the information to decide what experience I want.”

She added that including people with disabilities in trail assessment and information gathering can help land managers identify barriers. Some mobility devices can navigate rocks, roots, and steps; a narrow bridge or gate might stop someone instead.

“Five miles of the trail might work totally fine, and then there’s a bridge to cross some water, and they just threw down a 12-inch bridge, and after that, there’s five more miles of trail that work just fine,” Brett said. “But if you’ve never hung out with somebody with a disability, then you might not know what is truly a barrier.”

Taiwan’s Chunghwa Telecom Unveils 10 Content Investments, From ‘Gunshot’ to ‘Call of Lobster’

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Chunghwa Telecom, Taiwan’s largest telecom operator, announced 10 new investments at its “Chunghwa Telecom Content Slate and Partnership Showcase,” spanning film, TV and music projects as well as stakes in two production companies.

The company pitched the slate as a way to back Taiwanese original IP and link local creators with partners abroad. The event was held at the Taiwan Creative Content Fest (TCCF), which Chunghwa has sponsored for five consecutive years. Taiwan’s Minister of Culture Li Yuan attended, alongside Chunghwa chief business officer and senior executive VP Lin Wen-chiyh, consumer business group president Hu Hsueh-hai and representatives from the presenting teams. Related Stories Jane Fonda 'Didn't Dare Imagine' Democrats' Blue Tsunami: 'We Have to Block What's Bad and Fight For What's Good' Nicholas Galitzine Says Shooting 'Red, White & Royal Wedding' Was a 'Nice Reset' After World War II Movie 'The Mosquito Bowl'

“Chunghwa Telecom is like a pyramid,” Li said. “I am grateful that, beyond building a strong foundation through its thriving core business, the company is also willing to invest at the very top of the pyramid, supporting film and television professionals as they continue moving forward and creating even better work.”

The slate includes four scripted series: police crime drama “Gunshot,” made with PTS Taigi TV, the Taiwanese-language channel of public broadcaster PTS, and S11 Partners; family fantasy “Yesterday’s Daughter, Tomorrow’s Mother,” from producer Phoebe Ma and director Hung Tzu-peng; fantasy adventure “Guides to the World Below,” adapted from a popular Taiwanese comic; and “C’est la Vie,” an urban drama about food and women’s personal growth, with actress Esther Liu as executive producer. Chunghwa has also invested in “Next Z Chef,” billed as Taiwan’s first cooking competition show for young people.

Film investments include “Demon of the East: Black Condo,” a horror project tied to a cinematic universe that crosses four Asian countries, and “Call of Lobster,” which won the Chunghwa Telecom Award at TCCF’s 2024 pitching sessions and has since been selected for the Culture Ministry’s first international flagship cohort under its Great Voyage Project.

The investments also extend to music, including “Speak in Silence,” an Asian tour by Taiwanese indie band Shallow Levée. The tour will run to about 50 shows in Taiwan and eight other countries across Asia.

Alongside project financing, Chunghwa and Chunghwa Digital Cultural and Creative have taken equity positions in DaMou Entertainment and Horiza Creations. DaMou has produced series including “The World Between Us II” and “Wave Makers.” Horiza uses community engagement and crowdfunding to gauge what audiences want.

“Chunghwa Telecom is more than just a platform operator; we are also a driving force behind the long-term development of the content industry,” Lin said. “Moving forward, we will continue to connect funding, creative talent, and domestic and international partners, leveraging our group’s resources and industry partnerships to help Taiwanese content reach international markets.”
